	<item>
		<title>Build autonomous AI agents with Amazon Bedrock and Spring AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-autonomous-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-and-spring-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Viquar Khan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 01:18:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">52b0b0c3b44c826025ca35a812f53a3760ac1789</guid>

					<description>Learn how Java developers can build production-ready autonomous AI agents using Amazon Bedrock and Spring AI. This post walks through tool calling, conversation memory, resilience patterns, and multi-agent orchestration, then deploys the agent to AWS with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Java teams have established JVM tooling, continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines, and operational patterns, yet taking AI agents from prototype to production is hard: agent frameworks do not solve scalability, governance, security, and operational concerns by default. Amazon Bedrock and Spring AI close those gaps, so Java teams can build autonomous AI agents that plan, call tools and APIs, and maintain context across multi-turn conversations. Agentic AI is moving beyond prompt-response interactions toward autonomous systems that execute complex multi-step tasks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Java remains one of the widely used programming languages in enterprise software. Teams have existing CI/CD pipelines, operational expertise, and established patterns built around the JVM. &lt;a href="https://spring.io/projects/spring-ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Spring AI&lt;/a&gt; brings AI capabilities to the JVM through familiar Spring patterns: auto-configuration, dependency injection, and composable advisors. Developers can build AI agents using the same idioms they use for REST APIs, security, and data access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. With AgentCore, you can build, deploy, and operate AI agents at scale. It is framework-agnostic and model-agnostic: you bring your preferred agent framework and foundation model (FM). With AgentCore, you can access the production infrastructure you need: managed runtime, memory, browser automation, code execution, and identity management.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to build autonomous AI agents in Java using Spring AI and Amazon Bedrock with &lt;a href="https://github.com/spring-ai-community/spring-ai-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;spring-ai-agentcore&lt;/a&gt;. We explore the key implementation patterns for two systems. The first is a single-agent travel assistant that demonstrates tool calling and memory. The second is a multi-agent intelligent document processing (IDP) system that shows how specialist agents, a rules engine, and alerting work together.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along with this walkthrough, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Java&lt;/strong&gt; – JDK 21 (required)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Spring Boot&lt;/strong&gt; – 4.1 (with Spring AI 2.0 and spring-ai-agentcore 2.0)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Maven&lt;/strong&gt; – 3.9+.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Node.js&lt;/strong&gt; – 18+ (for the React frontend)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Terraform&lt;/strong&gt; – 1.5+.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Docker&lt;/strong&gt; – with Buildx for multi-architecture builds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/strong&gt; – v2, configured with credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;An AWS account&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-makes-a-system-an-agent"&gt;What makes a system an agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A chat completion call can generate text, but it can’t decide what to do next, reach into external systems, or remember what happened yesterday. An agent can, because it unites three capabilities:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Planning&lt;/strong&gt; – Given a goal, the model breaks it into steps, prioritizes them, and revises the plan as new information arrives.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool use&lt;/strong&gt; – Beyond generating text, the model can select and invoke external functions such as APIs, databases, and code interpreters to act in the world.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Memory&lt;/strong&gt; – Context carries across turns (short-term) and across sessions (long-term), so each decision builds on what came before.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) application has limited tool use (retrieval) but no planning loop. An agent has all three, operating in a goal-directed loop until the task is complete or it determines it cannot proceed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows these capabilities in an end-to-end agentic pipeline, from request to grounded answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-1.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-1.jpeg" alt="Reference architecture of an agentic pipeline from client request to grounded answer" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Agentic reference architecture. A request flows through the runtime, guardrails, classification, a specialist agent, tools, the foundation model, memory, rules, and human review to a grounded answer, with resilience and observability as cross-cutting concerns&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline has 11 stages. A client submits a document and question. The request passes through a runtime gateway for authentication and rate limiting, then input guardrails screen for harmful content. A router classifies the document and routes it to a specialist agent, which plans its workflow and calls tools, the Amazon Bedrock foundation model, memory, and a rules engine as needed. Low-confidence or flagged results go to a human review queue before the grounded answer returns to the client. Resilience, observability, logging, and least-privilege AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) apply across all stages.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution consists of two deployable systems that share common patterns:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Travel agent&lt;/strong&gt; – A single autonomous agent with tool calling (weather lookups, itinerary search), streaming responses, conversation memory, and resilience patterns. This is the recommended starting point.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IDP multi-agent system&lt;/strong&gt; – A multi-agent intelligent document processing platform with five specialist agents (Lawyer, Mortgage, Insurance, Tax, General), rules and workflow engine, alerting, and observability. This demonstrates production patterns at a scale.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both systems share the following architecture:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Spring Boot application with Spring AI ChatClient, Spring AI agentcore and Amazon Bedrock Converse API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, defines the contract (POST &lt;code&gt;/invocations&lt;/code&gt;, GET &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt;) for managed deployment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Conversation memory through AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, for both short-term and long-term context.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool calling with &lt;code&gt;@Tool&lt;/code&gt; annotated methods.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Structured output mapped to Java records.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;ARM64 container image optimized for AWS Graviton.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Modular Terraform for infrastructure provisioning.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The travel assistant is the recommended starting point. The following diagram illustrates how a Spring Boot agent interacts with AgentCore runtime, tools, memory, and models available on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-2.png" alt="Single-agent travel assistant connecting a Spring Boot application to AgentCore runtime and Amazon Bedrock" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Single-agent travel assistant. The Spring Boot application exposes both REST endpoints for the React UI and the AgentCore runtime contract, calling Amazon Bedrock through the Converse API while tool calling, streaming, and memory run inside one code base.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The travel assistant runs a single Spring Boot application with two entry points, the &lt;code&gt;/invocations&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt; contract required by AgentCore runtime. Spring AI orchestrates calls to Amazon Bedrock through the Converse API and resolves tool invocations locally inside the same process. Server-Sent Events stream tokens to the client as they are generated. AgentCore memory supplies both short-term context within a session and long-term context across sessions, so the agent retains conversation history without external state management.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the architecture for the IDP multi-agent system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-3.png" alt="IDP multi-agent architecture routing documents through specialist agents, a rules engine, and an alert service" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: IDP multi-agent system architecture. Specialist agents process documents through a shared ChatClient pattern, with results flowing through a rules engine and alert service.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The IDP multi-agent system routes documents through five specialist agents (Lawyer, Mortgage, Insurance, Tax, and General), each built on a shared &lt;code&gt;AbstractDocumentAgent&lt;/code&gt; template. A &lt;code&gt;ClassifierAgent&lt;/code&gt; reads each document and forwards it to the appropriate specialist. The specialist extracts structured JSON with confidence scores using the multimodal Converse API of Amazon Bedrock. A rules engine evaluates Spring Expression Language (SpEL) expressions from &lt;code&gt;application.yml&lt;/code&gt; against the extraction. &lt;code&gt;ALERT&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;BLOCK&lt;/code&gt; severity rules dispatch through the &lt;code&gt;AlertService&lt;/code&gt;. Low-confidence documents go to human review. Metrics, audit trail, resilience, and observability apply across all stages.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agent-collaboration-patterns"&gt;Agent collaboration patterns&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When multiple agents work together, how they coordinate determines system behavior. This solution uses three patterns:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram compares the three coordination patterns used in this solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-4.png" alt="Three agent collaboration patterns shown side by side: router, evaluator, and parallel fan-out" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Agent collaboration patterns&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three patterns shown side by side. The router dispatches each request to one specialist by document type. The evaluator passes specialist findings through a rules engine that applies to severity levels and dispatches alerts or blocks for human review. The parallel fan-out sends one request to multiple specialists concurrently and aggregates results before the rules engine runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Router pattern:&lt;/strong&gt; A lightweight orchestrator examines the incoming request and routes it to the appropriate specialist. In the IDP multi-agent system, the router inspects document type metadata and forwards it to the Lawyer, Mortgage, Insurance, Tax, or General agent. The router holds no domain logic. It is a dispatch layer only.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parallel fan-out (optional extension):&lt;/strong&gt; The base solution implements the router and evaluator patterns. The batch endpoint processes documents sequentially. When a document needs several perspectives at once, you can add a parallel fan-out as a drop-in extension. Dispatch to specialists concurrently and aggregate their results before the rules engine runs. Wall-clock time for the combined analysis is then bounded by the slowest single agent call rather than the sum of all calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Evaluator pattern: After specialists return their findings, a rules engine evaluates the combined output against configurable business rules. Rules that trigger &lt;code&gt;ALERT&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;BLOCK&lt;/code&gt; severity dispatch through a pluggable AlertService for human review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections walk you through the key implementation patterns, starting with the AgentCore runtime contract and progressing through tool calling, memory, resilience, and multi-agent orchestration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="fulfilling-the-agentcore-runtime-contract"&gt;Fulfilling the AgentCore runtime contract&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime manages agent lifecycle and scaling. For current rates, see Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing and Amazon Bedrock pricing. The runtime routes incoming requests to your agent and monitors its health. Your agent must expose two endpoints:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;POST &lt;code&gt;/invocations&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; – Receives requests and returns responses as JSON or Server-Sent Events (SSE) streaming.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GET &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; – Reports health status (&lt;code&gt;Healthy&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;HealthyBusy&lt;/code&gt;). Long-running tasks must signal busy status, or the runtime might scale down the agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how the Spring AI developer model maps onto AgentCore runtime, Memory, Browser, Code Interpreter, and Evaluations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-5.jpeg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-5.jpeg" alt="Spring AI components mapping through the spring-ai-agentcore SDK onto Amazon Bedrock AgentCore capabilities" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Spring AI AgentCore SDK on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Annotations, the ChatClient, tool callbacks, and advisors map through the spring-ai-agentcore SDK onto AgentCore runtime, AgentCore memory, AgentCore Browser, AgentCore Code Interpreter, and AgentCore Evaluations.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two layers connected by the spring-ai-agentcore SDK. The application layer uses Spring AI components: &lt;code&gt;@AgentCoreInvocation&lt;/code&gt; for endpoint wiring, &lt;code&gt;ChatClient&lt;/code&gt; for model interaction, &lt;code&gt;@Tool&lt;/code&gt; callbacks, and advisors for memory and guardrails. The infrastructure layer provides AgentCore runtime, AgentCore memory, AgentCore Browser, AgentCore Code Interpreter, and AgentCore Evaluations, all capabilities of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The SDK bridges both, so developers write standard Spring AI code while AgentCore handles production infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the Spring AI AgentCore SDK, a single annotation handles this contract automatically:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;@Service
public class TravelAgent {
    private final ChatClient chatClient;

    public TravelAgent(ChatClient.Builder builder) {
        this.chatClient = builder.build();
    }

    @AgentCoreInvocation
    public Flux&amp;lt;String&amp;gt; chat(PromptRequest request) {
        return chatClient.prompt()
                .user(request.prompt())
                .stream()
                .content();
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;@AgentCoreInvocation&lt;/code&gt; annotation auto-configures the &lt;code&gt;/invocations&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt; endpoints, handles JSON serialization, manages SSE framing for streaming responses, and reports health status. The same project also exposes REST endpoints for the React UI, so the agent serves both AgentCore runtime and a browser from one code base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A note on streaming: the preceding minimal example returns the model stream directly, which is ideal for a chat UI. When an agent makes multi-argument tool calls, assemble the response with a blocking &lt;code&gt;call()&lt;/code&gt; and then chunk the result for streaming, so the tool arguments bind reliably. The travel assistant serves both the AgentCore runtime and the React UI from one service method:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;@RestController
public class AgentController {
    private final AgentService agentService;

    public AgentController(AgentService agentService) {
        this.agentService = agentService;
    }

    // One annotation wires up POST /invocations + GET /ping, JSON/SSE framing, and health.
    @AgentCoreInvocation
    public Flux&amp;lt;String&amp;gt; invoke(PromptRequest request, AgentCoreContext context) {
        String sessionId = context.getHeader(AgentCoreHeaders.SESSION_ID);
        return agentService.streamChat(request.prompt(), sessionId,
                request.guardrailsEnabled(), request.memoryEnabled());
    }

    // The same app also serves the React UI from the same code base.
    @PostMapping(value = "/api/chat", produces = MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN_VALUE + ";charset=UTF-8")
    public Flux&amp;lt;String&amp;gt; chatStream(@Valid @RequestBody PromptRequest request) {
        return agentService.streamChat(request.prompt(), request.sessionId(),
                request.guardrailsEnabled(), request.memoryEnabled());
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementing-tool-calling"&gt;Implementing tool calling&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With tool calling, the model can invoke external functions during reasoning. In Spring AI, you annotate a method with &lt;code&gt;@Tool&lt;/code&gt; and the model decides when to call it and with what arguments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;@Tool(description = "Get a short weather forecast for a city")
public String getWeather(
        @ToolParam(description = "City name") String city) {
    return forecastService.lookup(city);
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You register tools on the ChatClient, and the model decides when to call them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;ChatClient chatClient = chatClientBuilder
        .defaultSystem(properties.getSystemPrompt())
        .defaultTools(new DateTimeTools(), new TravelTools())
        .build();&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For structured output, request a Java record type. For multimodal input, attach media to the user message:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;ProcessedDocument review = chatClient.prompt()
        .system(personaSystemPrompt)
        .user(u -&amp;gt; u.text(question).media(mimeType, fileResource))
        .call()
        .entity(ProcessedDocument.class);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; We verified this example with Anthropic Claude Sonnet 4.5 on Amazon Bedrock. Spring AI adds the record’s schema to the prompt and parses the reply back into the Java type. Behavior can differ for other model families, so refer to the Spring AI structured output documentation and the Amazon Bedrock documentation for current support. It is best-effort, meaning that models can occasionally return output that does not match the schema. For stricter results, use a provider with native schema enforcement or Spring AI validation and retry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model receives the tool definitions as part of its context, reasons about when a tool call would help satisfy the user request, and returns a structured tool call response. Spring AI intercepts this, invokes the annotated method, and feeds the result back to the model for further reasoning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="adding-conversation-memory"&gt;Adding conversation memory&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Real-world agents must remember what users said earlier in a conversation (working memory) and what they have learned over time (persistent memory). The Spring AI AgentCore SDK integrates with AgentCore memory through the advisor pattern: interceptors that enrich prompts with context before they reach the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;chatClient.prompt()
        .advisors(agentCoreMemory.advisors)
        .advisors(a -&amp;gt; a.param(
                ChatMemory.CONVERSATION_ID, "user:" + sessionId))
        .user(prompt)
        .stream()
        .content();&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The session ID becomes a conversation ID in actor:session form, so each user’s history stays isolated. For user-facing deployments, derive the actor from a verified OAuth 2.0 (JWT) identity rather than a client-supplied value, so users cannot access one another’s history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition, AgentCore memory offers long-term memory support, employing the following strategies to identify and capture key insights from conversations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Semantic&lt;/strong&gt; – Factual information about users (for example, “User works in finance”)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User preference&lt;/strong&gt; – Explicit settings and choices (for example, “Metric units preferred”)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Summary&lt;/strong&gt; – Condensed conversation history for continuity across sessions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Episodic&lt;/strong&gt; – Past interactions and lessons learned.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Long-term memory extraction is asynchronous and optional. You choose which strategies to enable when you create the memory, and AgentCore extracts and consolidates records in the background after each conversation, so it never blocks the agent’s response. Choose strategies by workload: use user preference for the travel assistant so it remembers units and seating choices. Use summary when sessions span many turns. Use episodic when the agent should reuse lessons from past tasks. And use semantic to retain durable facts about the user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configuring-the-rules-engine"&gt;Configuring the rules engine&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reading a document is only half the job. Acting on what it finds is the other half. The IDP multi-agent system includes a rules engine so operators can add business checks without touching code. This is an in-process rules evaluator that applies SpEL conditions to extraction results, not a workflow orchestration service such as AWS Step Functions. Rules live in &lt;code&gt;application.yml&lt;/code&gt; and each condition is a SpEL expression evaluated against the extraction:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;idp:
  review:
    enabled: ${REVIEW_ENABLED:true}
    confidence-threshold: ${REVIEW_CONFIDENCE_THRESHOLD:0.7}
    rules:
      - id: legal-unsigned
        agentType: LAWYER
        condition: "field('signaturePresent') == 'false' || missing('signaturePresent')"
        severity: ALERT
        message: "Document appears unsigned; get all parties to sign before relying on it."
      - id: mortgage-high-dti
        agentType: MORTGAGE
        condition: "field('dtiRatio') != '' &amp;amp;&amp;amp; T(java.lang.Double).parseDouble(field('dtiRatio')) &amp;gt; 0.43"
        severity: WARN
        message: "Debt-to-income ratio exceeds 0.43; likely needs manual underwriting."
      - id: low-confidence
        agentType: ALL
        condition: "confidence &amp;lt; 0.6"
        severity: WARN
        message: "Low extraction confidence; consider human verification."&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Severity levels control the engine’s behavior. &lt;code&gt;INFO&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;WARN&lt;/code&gt; are logged, while &lt;code&gt;ALERT&lt;/code&gt; dispatches through a pluggable &lt;code&gt;AlertService&lt;/code&gt;. The default logs and emits a metric, but you can substitute &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sns/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Notification Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon SNS), Slack, or email by providing your own bean. &lt;code&gt;BLOCK&lt;/code&gt; holds the document for manual review. Because rules are operator configuration rather than end user input, they provide a safe and auditable extension point. Adding new workflows is a configuration change, not a deployment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The evaluator pattern is implemented by the rules engine and a pluggable &lt;code&gt;AlertService&lt;/code&gt;. The default implementation logs the alert and emits a metric. You provide your own bean to route to Amazon SNS, Slack, or email:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-java"&gt;public class LoggingAlertService implements AlertService {
    @Override
    public void send(RuleResult result, AgentType agentType, ProcessedDocument document) {
        registry.counter("idp.alerts.sent",
                "severity", result.severity().name(), "type", agentType.name()).increment();
        logger.warn("ALERT [{}] rule={} type={} message=\"{}\"",
                result.severity(), result.ruleId(), agentType, result.message());
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="production-readiness-patterns"&gt;Production readiness patterns&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following patterns address common production requirements that prototypes typically skip.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="resilience"&gt;Resilience&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every model call uses a Resilience4j retry with exponential backoff, a circuit breaker that fails fast when model calls repeatedly fail or time out, and a timeout. Streaming paths emit a fallback message instead of a raw error. When traffic exceeds your account and Region service quotas, AgentCore runtime returns &lt;code&gt;HTTP 429&lt;/code&gt; and the retry handles it. For current limits, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/quotas.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore quotas documentation&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="twelve-factor-configurations"&gt;Twelve-factor configurations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every configurable value (Region, model ID, memory ID, timeouts, rate limits, system prompt) is an environment variable bound to a typed &lt;code&gt;@ConfigurationProperties&lt;/code&gt; class. The application is stateless, starts fast, drains in-flight turns on scale-down, and ships as one immutable ARM64 image promoted unchanged from development to production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="security-and-identity"&gt;Security and identity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Agentcore runtime uses AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) authentication by default. For user-facing agents, add an OAuth 2.0 (JWT) authorizer (such as Amazon Cognito) and derive the memory actor ID (actorId) from the verified identity. The IAM execution role follows least privilege:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) pull.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scoped Amazon CloudWatch Logs and AWS X-Ray.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock InvokeModel actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scoped workload access tokens (&lt;code&gt;GetWorkloadAccessTokenForJWT&lt;/code&gt;) for the agent’s workload identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore memory actions limited to the specific Memory resource ARN.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, enforces inbound authentication (acting as an OAuth 2.0 resource server that validates JWT bearer tokens through Cognito, Okta, Auth0, or your own OIDC provider) and supports configurable rate limiting at the gateway layer. Rate limits are fail-open by default, so they should complement your core security controls rather than replace them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="responsible-ai-controls"&gt;Responsible AI controls&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Autonomous agents that take real-world actions need safety controls as a first-class concern. The sample ships with a stand-in demonstration of a lightweight in-process pre-check called &lt;code&gt;GuardrailService&lt;/code&gt;. This service screens prompts for disallowed intent and prompt injection, toggled per request with the &lt;code&gt;guardrailsEnabled&lt;/code&gt; flag. Production deployments use Amazon Bedrock Guardrails as a standard control applied to every request, not a per-request toggle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To upgrade the demo service for production, front the agent with Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. Guardrails apply configurable policies outside the model call, covering both input and output:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Content filtering. Blocks harmful or off-topic content based on configurable thresholds for both prompts and responses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Denied topics. Prevents the agent from engaging with topics you define as out-of-scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sensitive information filters. Detects and redacts PII in both prompts and responses, supporting requirements such as GDPR and HIPAA.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For agents that retrieve external knowledge, enable grounding checks to reduce hallucination risk before the agent acts on tool calls with real-world side effects.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To wire Bedrock Guardrails into Spring AI, implement &lt;code&gt;CallAdvisor&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;StreamAdvisor&lt;/code&gt; functions that call the &lt;code&gt;ApplyGuardrail&lt;/code&gt; API and register it with &lt;code&gt;defaultAdvisors&lt;/code&gt; on the &lt;code&gt;ChatClient&lt;/code&gt;. Because policies live in the guardrail, security teams can update filters independently of the application release cycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="observability"&gt;Observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example instruments with the Micrometer Observation API (&lt;code&gt;@Observed&lt;/code&gt;), producing timers that become distributed trace spans when you add a tracing bridge (OTLP to AWS X-Ray). The system emits domain metrics:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;idp.documents.processed{type,status}
idp.extraction.confidence{type}
idp.alerts.sent{severity,type}
idp.processing.duration{type}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These metrics are exposed through &lt;code&gt;/actuator/prometheus&lt;/code&gt;. The application also emits structured JSON logs to standard output, which Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime captures in Amazon CloudWatch Logs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Optional extension that’s not implemented in this post: The sample includes the Micrometer OpenTelemetry bridge and OTLP exporter dependencies, but this post’s deployment does not provision an OpenTelemetry collector or enable trace sampling. You can add distributed tracing by setting a nonzero &lt;code&gt;TRACING_SAMPLING&lt;/code&gt; value, pointing &lt;code&gt;OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT&lt;/code&gt; to an OpenTelemetry or AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry collector, and configuring the collector to forward traces to AWS X-Ray.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Optional extension that’s not implemented in this post: The sample doesn’t implement an automated evaluation or optimization feedback loop. You can add one by persisting application metrics, traces, audit events, and human-review outcomes as an evaluation dataset. AgentCore evaluations or a separate evaluation pipeline can then compare versioned prompts, models, tools, and routing rules against quality, safety, latency, and cost thresholds. Promote changes only after evaluation and human approval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploying-the-solution"&gt;Deploying the solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-build-autonomous-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-and-spring-ai.git" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; contains two AI agent applications demonstrating different use cases for autonomous AI. This guide shows you how to deploy them to AWS using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the deployment architecture: a single Arm64 image promoted through modular Terraform to the AgentCore runtime, with an optional Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optional: running on Amazon EKS.&lt;/strong&gt; Teams already standardized on Kubernetes can run the same Arm64 image on Amazon EKS, using their existing cluster for scaling and health checks against the &lt;code&gt;/ping&lt;/code&gt; endpoint, with IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA) for scoped, least-privilege pod-level access to Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore memory. The container is identical, so nothing in the application changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/ML-20629-6.png" alt="Deployment architecture where Terraform provisions Amazon ECR, an IAM role, and AgentCore runtime, with an optional Amazon EKS path" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Deployment architecture. Modular Terraform provisions Amazon ECR, a least-privilege IAM execution role, and the Bedrock AgentCore runtime, with an optional Amazon EKS and IRSA path that reuses the same Arm64 image.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three stages: build and publish, Terraform, and runs on AWS. The CI pipeline packages the application into a linux/arm64 image and pushes it to Amazon ECR with immutable tags. Three Terraform modules provision the ECR repository, a least-privilege IAM execution role, and the AgentCore runtime. The agent runs serverlessly on AgentCore runtime, calling Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore memory. An optional Amazon EKS path reuses the same image with IRSA for scoped pod-level credentials.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow these steps to deploy either application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-clone-the-repository"&gt;Step 1: Clone the repository&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open your terminal and run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;git clone https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-build-autonomous-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-and-spring-ai.git&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-navigate-to-the-directory-for-the-agent-you-want-to-deploy"&gt;Step 2: Navigate to the directory for the agent you want to deploy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy autonomous agent: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd build-autonomous-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-and-spring-ai/code/autonomous-agent&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy IDP multi-agent system: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd build-autonomous-ai-agents-with-amazon-bedrock-and-spring-ai/code/idp-agent&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-configure-terraform-variables"&gt;Step 3: Configure Terraform variables&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying the infrastructure, configure your Terraform variables in the &lt;code&gt;terraform.tfvars&lt;/code&gt; file located in the terraform directory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Basic configuration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution uses default values for most settings, but you can customize the following in your &lt;code&gt;terraform.tfvars&lt;/code&gt; file:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Region: The AWS Region where resources are deployed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Environment: Environment ID (such as &lt;code&gt;dev&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;prod&lt;/code&gt;) used in resource naming.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;bedrock_model&lt;/code&gt;: Amazon Bedrock foundation model (for example: &lt;code&gt;global.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0&lt;/code&gt;). For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-run-the-deployment-script"&gt;Step 4: Run the deployment script&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Linux/macOS:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./scripts/deploy.sh dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Windows PowerShell:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-powershell"&gt;.\scripts\deploy.ps1 dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The deployment script does the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create an Amazon ECR repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Build the Java application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create and push a Docker container image.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create an IAM execution role.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy the AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-run-the-application"&gt;Step 5: Run the application&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once the deployment is complete, you can run the application using the following commands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Linux/macOS:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./scripts/run.sh dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Windows PowerShell:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-powershell"&gt;.\scripts\run.ps1 dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime scales to zero and bills per invocation, so an idle agent costs almost nothing. Your main variable cost is Amazon Bedrock token usage, plus a small amount for Amazon CloudWatch and Amazon ECR storage. For current rates, see Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing and Amazon Bedrock pricing. To avoid incurring future charges, tear down the infrastructure when you are finished:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Linux/macOS:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./scripts/cleanup.sh dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Windows PowerShell:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-powershell"&gt;.\scripts\cleanup.ps1 dev&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Delete any AgentCore memory resources and Amazon ECR images you no longer need using the AWS CLI.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to build autonomous AI agents in Java using Spring AI and Amazon Bedrock. We covered the three capabilities that turn a model into an agent: planning, tool use, and memory. We also showed how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore services carry an agent to production. The Spring AI AgentCore SDK maps these capabilities onto idiomatic Spring through annotations and a familiar &lt;code&gt;ChatClient&lt;/code&gt;. Finally, we outlined architecture patterns for multi-agent collaboration. These include the router pattern, the evaluator pattern implemented by the rules engine, and parallel fan-out as an optional extension.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key takeaway for Java teams: you do not need to leave your environment to build production-quality agents. The patterns you already trust (dependency injection, configuration properties, advisors, Actuator, Micrometer) carry directly into the agentic world. AgentCore supplies the runtime, memory, identity, and governance that production demands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As next steps, consider the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Connect enterprise systems through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway-transforming-enterprise-ai-agent-tool-development/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/2026-07-28/getting-started/intro" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, the fully managed RAG capability, and web search so the agent reasons over your data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implement Amazon Cognito JWT authentication with per-user memory isolation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Customize Amazon Bedrock Guardrails policies: add domain-specific denied topics, tighten content-filtering thresholds, and enable grounding checks tuned to your knowledge sources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use AgentCore Evaluations to score production traces and promote prompt or model changes after review.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each is an incremental addition to the foundation you have built here, not a rewrite.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>OpenAI Codex on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/openai-codex-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Shivi Bhatia]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 23:35:47 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">faefdbed0491df7ffbfea5b7e93f3979e26376c0</guid>

					<description>A practical guide for enterprises rolling out OpenAI Codex to developers on AWS, with Amazon Bedrock as the model backend, covering the access, governance, and cost decisions that platform, security, and finance teams need to align on.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Developers increasingly use Codex with Amazon Bedrock to explore unfamiliar code, make changes, and handle repetitive engineering work. For platform teams, the challenge is to make those tools available without creating a second model-access, identity, and operations stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Codex can use Amazon Bedrock for inference with supported OpenAI models. In this post, we show how to fit that path into the AWS identity, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), Regional, and observability practices your organization already uses. Codex still runs on the developer workstation. The main design choice is how its model requests reach Amazon Bedrock and where your organization applies policy and operational controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We begin with a small, least-privilege pilot and work through the checks that matter before access expands. The examples use GPT-5.6 Sol. Before starting the pilot, review &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; to confirm that Sol, Terra, or Luna is available in the AWS Region and inference path you plan to use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended default:&lt;/strong&gt; Start with Native AWS Access for a small, least-privilege pilot. Choose AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, when you want AWS managed gateway infrastructure for routing or Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. Choose an LLM Gateway when a platform team wants to enforce controls in the request path. A custom integration is the advanced version of the LLM Gateway pattern, not a quick start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The examples come from the open source Guidance for Codex on AWS repository. It includes quick starts for Native AWS Access, AgentCore Gateway, LiteLLM on ECS, Portkey, the AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry (ADOT), and the Amazon CloudWatch setup used later in the post. Choose the quick start that matches your architecture, use it for the pilot, and adapt it to your environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choose-your-rollout-path"&gt;Choose your rollout path&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Authentication is only one part of an enterprise rollout. Decide who can use the service and which models and Regions are approved. Also define where configuration lives, what telemetry to collect, how usage is allocated, and which skills or Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers are allowed. The following table maps those needs to an access path and an owning team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose the access path that provides the controls you need now. The most straightforward path that meets the requirement usually has the lowest operational overhead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-rules"&gt;Decision rules&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Native AWS Access is the usual starting point when AWS identity and IAM controls are enough for the pilot.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. Within the platform, AgentCore Gateway fits teams that want AWS managed gateway infrastructure for routing, Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, or related controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A large language model (LLM) Gateway fits organizations that already operate a gateway or want to enforce controls in the request path. Building a custom gateway is the advanced version of this option.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram compares where identity, routing, policy, telemetry, and operational ownership sit across the three rollout paths.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram comparing Native AWS Access, AgentCore Gateway, and LLM Gateway paths from Codex to Amazon Bedrock." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Codex runs on the developer workstation in each scenario. Native AWS Access uses AWS IAM Identity Center, a configured AgentCore Gateway can apply managed routing and Guardrails, and a configured LLM Gateway can apply request-path controls&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="native-aws-access"&gt;Native AWS Access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If Native AWS Access fits the pilot, the repository’s &lt;a href="https://github.com/openai-on-aws/guidance-codex/blob/main/docs/QUICKSTART_NATIVE_AWS_ACCESS.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Native AWS Access quick start&lt;/a&gt; provides the complete procedure and supporting files. The following abbreviated walkthrough focuses on the sequence and the checks that matter most.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-your-workforce-identity-provider-for-sign-in"&gt;Use your workforce identity provider for sign-in&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Employees continue to sign in through the identity provider they already use. In this example, Okta handles authentication through Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) and provisions users and groups into IAM Identity Center through System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM). IAM Identity Center then issues the temporary AWS credentials used by Codex. The identity provider is not configured in Codex.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add each pilot user to a dedicated group in the workforce identity system and provision that group to IAM Identity Center. Keep the assignment limited to the pilot.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose an Amazon Bedrock Region and an OpenAI model available to the pilot identity, plus an inference profile if the selected API requires one. Check Sol, Terra, and Luna separately for the chosen account, Region, and inference path.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grant only the Amazon Bedrock model inference permissions used by Codex, any direct validation commands, and the selected observability destination.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prepare a non-production repository and name a rollback owner.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="configure"&gt;Configure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pilot has three checks. First, sign in using the IAM Identity Center profile configured for the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) and call Amazon Bedrock Runtime directly. Next, point Codex to the approved model. Finally, run a safe Codex task. The direct call isolates AWS access problems. The final step confirms the Codex configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Sign in and validate Amazon Bedrock Runtime access.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sso login --profile codex-bedrock
export AWS_PROFILE=codex-bedrock
aws bedrock-runtime converse
--model-id us.openai.gpt-5.6-sol
--messages '[{"role":"user","content":[{"text":"Reply with OK"}]}]'
--region us-east-2&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This command creates a short-lived AWS session and checks whether the profile can invoke the chosen model in the chosen Region. If it fails, resolve the profile, Region, model or inference-profile access, or IAM permissions before configuring Codex.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Configure the Codex provider.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;# ~/.codex/config.toml (or managed user configuration)
model_provider = "amazon-bedrock"
model = "openai.gpt-5.6-sol"

[model_providers.amazon-bedrock.aws]
profile = "codex-bedrock"
region = "us-east-2"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model value is the Codex model ID. The profile selects the AWS IAM Identity Center session, and region tells Codex which AWS Region to use. Keep these settings in user-level or managed configuration so a repository cannot change the provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="verify-the-pilot-and-confirm-rollback"&gt;Verify the pilot and confirm rollback&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before adding users, make sure the pilot reaches the approved model, produces the expected records, and can be disabled cleanly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Identity: &lt;code&gt;aws sts get-caller-identity&lt;/code&gt; returns the expected IAM Identity Center assumed-role session.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model access: The pilot profile invokes the approved model through the API and Region selected for the test.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Codex access: The user completes a safe, read-only task in a non-production repository, and the expected request-path evidence is available.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Evidence and rollback: The identity, invocation, audit, and optional telemetry records are present. Remove the configuration or IAM Identity Center assignment, handle any active session according to your procedure or wait for it to expire, and verify that a new invocation is denied.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="watch-the-native-aws-access-walkthrough"&gt;Watch the Native AWS Access walkthrough&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough shows the complete pilot sequence: workforce sign-in, direct Amazon Bedrock Runtime validation, Codex provider configuration, and a safe test task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-144260-1" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21633/OpenAI_Codex_on_Bedrock_Pilot_Walkthrough.mp4?_=1"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2: Native AWS Access walkthrough covering workforce sign-in, Amazon Bedrock Runtime validation, Codex provider configuration, and a safe pilot task&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the video to verify the order of the checks and the evidence to collect before adding more pilot users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="run-a-two-user-identity-isolation-check"&gt;Run a two-user identity-isolation check&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two users are required to test identity isolation. A single user shows that sign-in works. A second user tests whether attribution remains distinct. Have two pilot users sign in separately and run the same harmless task. Their assumed-role sessions and audit records, and telemetry records if configured, should remain distinct.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how separate pilot identities should produce separate AWS sessions, request evidence, and optional telemetry while using the same approved Codex and Amazon Bedrock path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-3.png" alt="Two-user pilot diagram showing separate IAM Identity Center sessions, Codex requests to Amazon Bedrock, identity evidence, and optional telemetry." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Use trusted identity enrollment for attribution. A local collector can add approved identity context only when it is deployed and configured as part of a managed enrollment process&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="gateway-paths-for-request-path-controls"&gt;Gateway paths for request-path controls&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Gateway is an option when you want AWS managed gateway infrastructure for centralized routing or Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. If your organization already runs an LLM Gateway or wants to enforce controls in the request path, use that gateway instead. Validate the selected gateway before exposing it to pilot users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="llm-gateway-validate-before-a-pilot"&gt;LLM Gateway: Validate before a pilot&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check that the gateway accepts the Codex request format.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use the sign-in or token flow defined by the gateway team.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Before inviting pilot users, test model routing, monitoring, failure handling, and rollback.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="custom-enterprise-integration"&gt;Custom enterprise integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A custom enterprise integration is the most involved form of the LLM Gateway pattern. Consider it only when Native AWS Access, AgentCore Gateway, and an existing gateway cannot meet a specific requirement. The result is another service to design, secure, test, support, and operate, so platform engineering and security need to own it from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="observe-govern-and-allocate-cost"&gt;Observe, govern, and allocate cost&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Codex OpenTelemetry, Amazon Bedrock service records, AWS identity records, and billing data answer different questions. Codex OpenTelemetry describes client activity. Amazon Bedrock service metrics and model invocation logs show service-side activity when enabled. AWS identity records and the AWS Cost and Usage Report (CUR) provide identity and billed-usage evidence. Correlate them for the path you deploy, and do not present token volume as a cost report.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Define what code, prompts, files, and tool output Codex might send through the selected model path. Exclude secrets and restricted data unless the organization has approved that data flow. Require developers to review and test generated changes before merge. Telemetry does not establish correctness or safety.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="opentelemetry-metrics-from-codex"&gt;OpenTelemetry metrics from Codex&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For observability, this post uses the ADOT collector configuration and CloudWatch dashboard from the repository’s &lt;a href="https://github.com/openai-on-aws/guidance-codex/blob/main/docs/operate-monitoring.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;monitoring and operations guidance&lt;/a&gt;. Deploy that configuration to each managed pilot device, then run a safe Codex task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository provides the configuration files. Follow its deployment instructions to install the approved ADOT collector and configure its AWS authentication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;# ~/.codex/config.toml or managed user configuration
[otel]
environment = "pilot"
log_user_prompt = false
metrics_exporter = { "otlp-http" = { endpoint = "http://127.0.0.1:4318/v1/metrics", protocol = "binary" } }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Validate the repository path: run a safe Codex task and confirm that the expected operational metrics appear on the managed CloudWatch dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Telemetry plane&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Examples&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribution boundary&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Codex OpenTelemetry metrics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Token usage, turns, API requests, request duration, tool calls, session source.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-user and team dashboard grouping only after the local collector adds validated identity attributes.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock service metrics&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Invocations, latency, input tokens, output tokens, client errors, server errors.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use the documented namespace, metric names, and dimensions for the selected Runtime, Mantle, or AgentCore path.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS identity evidence, model invocation logs, and CUR&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Assumed-role single sign-on (SSO) principal plus billed Bedrock line items.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;For Native IAM Identity Center access, CUR 2.0 with caller-identity allocation can expose the SSO principal. Gateway paths require gateway telemetry and spend records for end-user allocation.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-cloudwatch-dashboard-and-evidence"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch dashboard and evidence&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon CloudWatch Coding Agent Insights managed dashboard in the Guidance for Codex on AWS repository shows Codex operational metrics collected through ADOT. Use it to understand client activity, and use AWS identity, audit, and billing records separately when you need attribution or cost evidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Question&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decision&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evidence to keep&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Who invoked the model?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use the AWS identity/audit source for the path you selected.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pilot identity record and request correlation, where available.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What did it cost?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use billing data for actual charge and label any inferred allocation clearly.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CUR source, allocation method, and known uncertainty.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Can we stop unsafe or expensive behavior?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use alarms for detection. Use IAM and managed client controls for their respective access and action boundaries. Use gateway policy when request-path enforcement, such as hard budgets or rate limits, is required.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Control test results and rollback procedure.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="watch-the-codex-otel-and-cloudwatch-walkthrough"&gt;Watch the Codex OTel and CloudWatch walkthrough&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The walkthrough shows how to query Codex OpenTelemetry metrics and confirm that the expected token and request data appears in Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-144260-2" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21633/Codex_Prompt_OTel_CloudWatch_Walkthrough.mp4?_=2"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4: Codex OpenTelemetry metrics shown in an Amazon CloudWatch dashboard&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The example validates the aliased metric result in Amazon CloudWatch Query Studio and shows how the dashboard represents Codex client activity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="standardize-team-behavior"&gt;Standardize team behavior&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the infrastructure path is working, turn to the developer experience. Repository guidance, managed plugins, hooks, and approved MCP servers can make the rollout consistent without asking each team to assemble its own setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="plugins"&gt;Plugins&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organization-owned plugins are a practical way to distribute shared skills, hooks, and approved MCP integrations. Give each plugin an owner and version, review changes before release, and publish it only through approved sources. Test updates with a pilot group and keep a documented removal path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A plugin might include an MCP server configuration, but that does not make every bundled tool approved. Allow only the servers and tools the organization has reviewed, with user confirmation where it is needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hooks"&gt;Hooks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hooks can be declared in &lt;code&gt;hooks.json&lt;/code&gt; or directly in configuration. Keep the documented event casing, and deploy organization-owned policy commands through approved endpoint tooling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;[[hooks.PreToolUse]]
matcher = "^Bash$"

[[hooks.PreToolUse.hooks]]
type = "command"
command = "/usr/local/bin/company-pre-tool-policy"
timeout = 30&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use PreToolUse, PostToolUse, or SessionStart where they solve a specific need. Before wider deployment, test allow, deny, timeout, and disconnected-endpoint behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mcp"&gt;MCP&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An MCP server connects Codex to another system, so each server creates a separate trust boundary. Limit access to the users and tools that need it, manage credentials through an approved OAuth or secrets process, and require confirmation for tools that change external systems. Keep MCP credentials out of the model-provider configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="roll-out-in-phases"&gt;Roll out in phases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram defines the scope and evidence required to advance from preparation through pilot, expansion, and organization-wide standardization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21633-5.png" alt="Phased rollout diagram showing Prepare, Pilot, Expand, and Standardize stages with entry and exit criteria." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Start with a controlled pilot, expand after validating the access path and operational evidence, and standardize the rollout after the operating model is proven&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="administrator-launch-checklist"&gt;Administrator launch checklist&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record the selected path, owning team, Region, and model in the change record.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Limit the pilot identity to the required permissions, and document how issued credentials or tokens are handled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a non-production test that covers sign-in, model availability, and one safe Codex task.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check telemetry and audit output from the deployed version rather than assuming the example applies unchanged.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Separate billed amounts from estimates in cost reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Exercise recovery: remove the configuration or assignment, handle any active session through the approved procedure or wait for it to expire, and verify that a new invocation is denied.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the pilot ends or access must be disabled, remove the rollout resources that are no longer required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Remove the Codex model-provider and telemetry configuration from managed devices. Remove the IAM Identity Center pilot assignment or gateway access, revoke pilot tokens and secrets, and handle active sessions according to the approved procedure. Uninstall or disable the ADOT collector, and delete the pilot CloudWatch dashboard and alarms if they are not shared with another workload. Verify cleanup by confirming that a new model invocation is denied and that retired telemetry resources receive no new data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configuration-boundaries"&gt;Configuration boundaries&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep model-provider, credential, and organization telemetry settings in user-level or managed configuration. Project-local &lt;code&gt;.codex/config.toml&lt;/code&gt; is repository-scoped configuration: Review it as code, but do not treat it as an enterprise enforcement boundary. AGENTS.md is useful for durable repository guidance, such as test commands and coding conventions, but it is not an enforcement boundary either.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setting type&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended location&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reason&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model provider, credentials, Region&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User-level or managed configuration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;These are endpoint and enterprise choices, not repository defaults.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OpenTelemetry exporter&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;User-level or managed configuration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Telemetry destination and data handling are organization controls.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Repository instructions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AGENTS.md&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Carries durable guidance but is not an enforcement boundary.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Permission requirements&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Organization-managed &lt;code&gt;requirements.toml&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Constrains supported security-sensitive client choices.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusions-and-next-steps"&gt;Conclusions and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Codex can use Amazon Bedrock for inference with supported OpenAI models. Start with the least-complex access path that meets the requirement, prove the identity and model path with a small pilot, and add gateway or client controls only when they solve a specific need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Gateway provides an AWS managed gateway option for routing or Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. Choose an LLM Gateway when a platform team wants to enforce controls in the request path. Build a custom integration only when the available paths cannot meet a documented requirement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Expand in stages and use evidence from each stage to decide what comes next. The Guidance for Codex on AWS repository contains the maintained configurations and validation procedures used in this post.&lt;/p&gt;
