		<item>
		<title>Accelerating generative AI delivery with reusable assets</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-generative-ai-delivery-with-reusable-assets/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sabine Khan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 19:19:21 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Generative AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">942893d15c6ed4a3b822a98a54e996da0a60453b</guid>

					<description>Every generative AI team keeps rebuilding the same components: RAG pipelines, document processors, and agent scaffolding. This post shows how to access a curated catalog of 23 production-ready, open source reusable assets from the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center, with real delivery results from DoorDash, Lyft, and Ricoh.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;If you’re building generative AI solutions, reusable assets can help you stop rebuilding components that already exist: Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines, document processors, agent scaffolding, and so on. This has a compounding cost: time not spent solving the customer’s actual problem.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows you how to access a curated catalog of 23 production-ready assets from the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center, now part of AWS Frontier AI Engineering &amp;amp; Services. These assets can reduce your development time from months to weeks, with evidence from companies like &lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/solutions/case-studies/doordash-bedrock-case-study/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DoorDash&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="http://lyft.com/blog/posts/aws-and-lyft-bringing-agentic-ai-to-life-for-riders-and-drivers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Lyft&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-ricoh-built-a-scalable-intelligent-document-processing-solution-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ricoh&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Since 2023, the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center has published open source reusable assets. These production-grade building blocks give you a validated foundation to start from instead of a blank file. These assets have powered customer solutions delivering measurable results: 50x increases in testing capacity, 90 percent reductions in engineering hours, and deployment timelines compressed from months to weeks. In this post, you will learn why teams keep rebuilding generative AI components, what makes an asset truly reusable, how companies like DoorDash and Ricoh achieved measurable results by using these assets, and how to access and deploy 23 open source assets from the catalog. For a complete list of all available assets, see the asset catalog at the end of this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-why-teams-keep-rebuilding"&gt;The problem: Why teams keep rebuilding&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three root causes drive redundant development across generative AI teams:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No single source of truth&lt;/strong&gt; – Assets scatter across repositories, wikis, and drives. Builders can’t find what exists, so they rebuild from scratch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No quality signal&lt;/strong&gt; – Without verification of production readiness, security reviews, or maintenance status, builders have no way to trust existing solutions and default to writing their own code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No reuse culture&lt;/strong&gt; – Sharing remains informal and ungoverned. What one team builds stays with that team, and problems get solved repeatedly.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cost of rebuilding compounds. Each redundant build consumes engineering hours, delays customer delivery, and creates maintenance burden. Even AI-generated code requires production hardening, security reviews, testing at scale, and ongoing maintenance. These assets arrive already validated, security-reviewed, and production-grade, avoiding that verification burden. When teams finally reach production, they’ve spent weeks on undifferentiated heavy lifting, work that should have been reusable from day one. The AWS Frontier AI Engineering &amp;amp; Services resolves this challenge through reusable assets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-a-reusable-asset-actually-is"&gt;What a reusable asset actually is&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A reusable asset isn’t a code snippet or a sample. It’s a production-grade, composable building block that implements a discrete generative AI capability. Reusable assets are validated for quality and security before publication. They include clear deployment guides for discoverability and are instrumented to track adoption across customer engagements. These assets are maintained across their lifecycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Examples from the catalog include RAG pipeline components, document ingestion modules, agent scaffolding templates, evaluation frameworks, and prompt management patterns. The assets are tested in production environments, reviewed for security compliance, and refined based on real customer feedback.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is the difference between a one-off artifact and a building block you can trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="real-results-customer-success-stories"&gt;Real results: Customer success stories&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value of reusable assets isn’t theoretical. Here’s what happens when teams build on proven foundations instead of starting from scratch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="contact-center-genai-agent-doordash-and-lyft"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent: DoorDash and Lyft&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;According to the AWS case study, &lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/solutions/case-studies/doordash-bedrock-case-study/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DoorDash&lt;/a&gt; built a fully voice-operated self-service generative AI contact center solution using the &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/a&gt; asset. As reported, the solution handles over 100,000 calls per day with 2.5-second response latency, achieving a 50x increase in testing capacity. Using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; reduced development time by 50 percent. The team reached live testing readiness in 2 months.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="http://lyft.com/blog/posts/aws-and-lyft-bringing-agentic-ai-to-life-for-riders-and-drivers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Lyft&lt;/a&gt; used the same &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/a&gt; asset to bring agentic AI to life for riders and drivers, demonstrating the asset’s versatility across different customer engagement models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Contact Center GenAI Agent provides automated question answering capabilities for both text and voice channels. It employs a RAG architecture to facilitate multi-turn conversations while maintaining context. The solution integrates with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/connect/latest/adminguide/what-is-amazon-connect.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Connect&lt;/a&gt; and uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, a fully managed RAG capability, for hybrid search and metadata filtering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learn more:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/solutions/case-studies/doordash-bedrock-case-study/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DoorDash Case Study&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://lyft.com/blog/posts/aws-and-lyft-bringing-agentic-ai-to-life-for-riders-and-drivers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Lyft Case Study&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-generative-ai-agents-in-your-contact-center-for-voice-and-chat-using-amazon-connect-amazon-lex-and-amazon-bedrock-knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI agent&lt;/a&gt; blog post&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="genai-intelligent-document-processing-ricoh"&gt;GenAI Intelligent Document Processing: Ricoh&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ricoh serves major health insurance payers, managed care organizations, and healthcare providers. These customers process hundreds of thousands of critical documents each month, including insurance claims, grievances, appeals, and clinical records. As Ricoh scaled to serve more healthcare customers, they needed a standardized, multi-tenant document processing approach. That approach had to onboard new implementations without requiring unique development and tuning for each one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using the &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/accelerated-intelligent-document-processing-on-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerated Intelligent Document Processing on AWS&lt;/a&gt; asset as a foundation, Ricoh built a standardized, multi-tenant solution for automated document classification and extraction. This transformed their document processing from a custom-engineering bottleneck into a scalable, repeatable service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Results: As documented in the published case study, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-ricoh-built-a-scalable-intelligent-document-processing-solution-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ricoh&lt;/a&gt; achieved a 7-fold throughput increase, from 10,000–70,000 documents monthly. Engineering hours per deployment dropped by over 90 percent (80 hours to under 5). Onboarding time fell from 4–6 weeks to 2–3 days. The system now processes 1,000 documents in minutes during traffic spikes, delivering over 1,900 potential annual person-hours in savings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The GenAI IDP Accelerator, introduced in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-intelligent-document-processing-with-generative-ai-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;this 2025 blog post&lt;/a&gt;, is a scalable, serverless solution for automated document processing and information extraction using AWS services. This system combines optical character recognition (OCR) with generative AI to convert unstructured documents into structured data at scale. The AWS Frontier AI Engineering &amp;amp; Services continuously maintains the assets as well.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learn more:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Blog post: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-intelligent-document-processing-with-generative-ai-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS GenAI Intelligent Document Processing (IDP) Accelerator&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Blog post: &lt;a href="http://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-ricoh-built-a-scalable-intelligent-document-processing-solution-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How Ricoh built a scalable intelligent document processing solution on AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Source Code: &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/accelerated-intelligent-document-processing-on-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accelerated-intelligent-document-processing-on-aws&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="new-assets-building-the-future-of-agentic-ai"&gt;New assets: Building the future of agentic AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reusable assets become more valuable as teams shift focus from infrastructure to business logic. Two recently launched assets demonstrate how reusable building blocks can accelerate the development of next-generation agentic applications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fullstack-agentcore-solution-template-fast"&gt;Fullstack AgentCore Solution Template (FAST)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/awslabs/fullstack-solution-template-for-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;The Fullstack AgentCore Solution Template (FAST)&lt;/a&gt; is a starter project repository. With FAST, you can quickly deploy a secured React frontend connected to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model. In this implementation, FAST uses a React-based frontend with the managed infrastructure of AgentCore to accelerate building full-stack agentic applications from weeks to days.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;FAST deploys in under 30 minutes using infrastructure as code (IaC), with built-in integrations for AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, along with Gateway, Code Interpreter, and Observability. Authentication uses Amazon Cognito, configured with enterprise security best practices, and the template is designed for AI-assisted development with extensive documentation and steering docs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With FAST, you can access a complete reference architecture that shows you how the pieces integrate, with a working full stack application that you can learn from and build upon. With the modular design, you can swap out major components (identity providers, frontend frameworks, hosting solutions, or agent frameworks) as your requirements evolve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learn more:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/awslabs/fullstack-solution-template-for-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;FullStack AgentCore Solution Template&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-agentic-application-development-with-a-full-stack-starter-template-for-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerate agentic application development with a full-stack starter template for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentic-chatbot-accelerator"&gt;Agentic Chatbot Accelerator&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agentic-chatbot-accelerator" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic Chatbot Accelerator&lt;/a&gt; is a web application deployment solution that uses the AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) for infrastructure as code so you can create agentic chatbots. Built on Strands Agents and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, this solution streamlines the development of agentic chatbot applications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The accelerator provides rapid deployment using AWS CDK, with scalable agent runtime management through Strands Agents and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. It is configurable and extensible for diverse chatbot use cases, with pre-configured patterns for authentication, hosting, and scaling, validated across customer deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Learn more:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agentic-chatbot-accelerator" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic Chatbot Accelerator&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-using-an-asset-from-the-library"&gt;Walkthrough: Using an asset from the library&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Let’s walk through a concrete example of selecting and using the &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/a&gt; asset from the open source catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow this walkthrough, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create AWS CloudFormation stacks, Amazon Connect instances, Amazon Bedrock resources, and Amazon Quick Sight dashboards.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) installed and configured with appropriate credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Connect instance (or permissions to create one).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to Anthropic Claude Sonnet and Anthropic Claude Haiku on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Jupyter notebook environment for validation testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-scenario"&gt;The scenario&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You’re building a customer support solution that needs to handle both voice and text interactions, answer questions using your company’s knowledge base, and escalate complex issues to human agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="finding-the-asset"&gt;Finding the asset&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Navigate to &lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/a&gt; on GitHub. The README provides a clear overview of capabilities, architecture, and deployment steps. The following figure shows the solution architecture for the Contact Center GenAI Agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20608-1-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-20608-1-3.png" alt="Architecture of the Contact Center GenAI Agent solution integrating Amazon Connect and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture for the Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="evaluating-the-asset"&gt;Evaluating the asset&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The asset includes security-reviewed code with AWS CDK infrastructure as code, integration with Amazon Connect for voice and chat, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, a fully managed RAG capability, for question answering. An automated testing framework and conversation analytics dashboard are built in.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Responsible-AI controls are integral to the architecture. Amazon Bedrock Guardrails enforces production-grade safety controls, including hallucination detection, content filtering, and topic-level boundaries, making sure agents operate within defined safety parameters from day one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository shows active maintenance, comprehensive documentation, and links to customer success stories (DoorDash, Lyft) that validate production readiness.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploying-the-solution"&gt;Deploying the solution&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deployment follows four steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the repository and install dependencies.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy two AWS CloudFormation templates: one for the Knowledge Base stack and one for the RAG solution stack.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Integrate with Amazon Connect using the pre-built contact flows included in the repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Validate the deployment using the included Jupyter notebooks.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full process takes under an hour. The solution also includes an Amazon Quick Sight dashboard for real-time conversation analytics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For detailed deployment instructions and prerequisites, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent?tab=readme-ov-file#deploy-and-test-the-solution" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deployment guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources created during this walkthrough. The deployment uses two AWS CloudFormation stacks, so deletion follows the reverse order:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the RAG solution stack from the AWS CloudFormation console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Knowledge Base stack from the AWS CloudFormation console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the Amazon Connect contact flows created during integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Amazon Quick Sight dashboard and any associated SPICE datasets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For detailed cleanup instructions, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent?tab=readme-ov-file#deploy-and-test-the-solution" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deployment guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-outcome"&gt;The outcome&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instead of spending weeks building a contact center solution from scratch, you can deploy a security-reviewed, tested foundation in days. Your team focuses on customizing the knowledge base, refining prompts for your domain, and integrating with your existing systems. This is the high-value work that differentiates your solution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Potential time saved:&lt;/strong&gt; 4–6 weeks of development time&lt;br&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What you focus on instead:&lt;/strong&gt; Domain-specific customization, prompt engineering, business logic integration&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="access-to-the-aws-generative-ai-innovation-center-open-source-assets"&gt;Access to the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center open source assets&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can access 23 reusable assets as open source on GitHub and AWS Labs. Each asset includes deployment guides, sample code, and documentation to help you get started quickly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="document-processing-and-extraction"&gt;Document processing and extraction&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-solutions-library-samples/accelerated-intelligent-document-processing-on-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GenAI Intelligent Document Processing (GenAIDP)&lt;/a&gt; – Scalable, serverless solution for automated document processing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-multi-lingual-document-processor" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Multi-lingual Document Processor (MDP)&lt;/a&gt; – Transforms complex multi-modal documents into structured text.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/intelligent-document-processing-with-amazon-bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Intelligent Document Processing with Generative AI&lt;/a&gt; – Extract information from custom documents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/extract-info-from-complex-docs-with-amazon-bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Extracting Information From Complex Documents&lt;/a&gt; – Hands-on workshop for document extraction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-comprehend-automated-pdf-prelabeling-tool" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Semi-Structured Documents Automatic Pre-Labeling Tool&lt;/a&gt; – Automates pre-labeling for Amazon Comprehend.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="contact-center-and-voice"&gt;Contact center and voice&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/contact-center-genai-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center GenAI Agent&lt;/a&gt; – Automated question answering for voice and text channels.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-transcribe-live-call-analytics" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Live Call Analytics with Agent Assist (LCA)&lt;/a&gt; – Real-time generative AI for customer and agent voice calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-transcribe-post-call-analytics" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Contact Center: Post Call Analytics (PCA)&lt;/a&gt; – Generative AI analysis of post-call recordings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-transcribe-live-meeting-assistant" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Live Meeting Assistant (LMA)&lt;/a&gt; – Captures audio from browser-based meetings with transcription and insights.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/recorded-voice-insight-extraction-webapp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Recorded Voice Insight Extraction Webapp (ReVIEW)&lt;/a&gt; – Upload recordings and use generative AI to answer questions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentic-ai-and-chatbots"&gt;Agentic AI and chatbots&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/awslabs/fullstack-solution-template-for-agentcore" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Fullstack AgentCore Solution Template (FAST)&lt;/a&gt; – Deploy secured React frontend with AgentCore backend.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-agentic-chatbot-accelerator" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic Chatbot Accelerator&lt;/a&gt; – Web application deployment for agentic chatbots.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/fashion-assistant-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Fashion Assistant Agent&lt;/a&gt; – Fashion assistant built with Amazon Titan on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-langgraph-nova-autobi" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Natural Language Database Agent&lt;/a&gt; – Query databases using natural language with LangGraph.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="rag-and-knowledge-bases"&gt;RAG and knowledge bases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-knowledge-base-terraform" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploy Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases using Terraform&lt;/a&gt; – Infrastructure as code for Knowledge Bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/rag-with-human-support" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Leveraging the User to Improve Agents in RAG Use Cases&lt;/a&gt; – Custom LangChain tool for conversational RAG.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/finetune-bge-embeddings-blog" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reranker Fine-Tuning and Deployment&lt;/a&gt; – Fine-tune BAAI BGE Reranker for improved retrieval.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-memorydb-for-redis-samples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Durable Semantic Caching in Amazon MemoryDB&lt;/a&gt; – Persistent semantic caching for GenAI applications.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="development-and-migration"&gt;Development and migration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-c-java-migration-nova-premier-strands" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;C to Java Code Conversion&lt;/a&gt; – Agentic workflow for legacy code migration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/prompt-migration-for-large-language-model-agility" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI Migration Framework&lt;/a&gt; – Systematic framework for large language model (LLM) migration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-finetune-modenBert-for-intent-classification" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Fine-tuning ModernBERT for Intent Classification&lt;/a&gt; – Create smart LLM routing systems.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="infrastructure-and-operations"&gt;Infrastructure and operations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/enable-bedrock-logging-using-cloudformation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock Logging Automation&lt;/a&gt; – Automate logging setup for Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://github.com/aws-samples/sample-data-analyst-bi" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data Analyst BI&lt;/a&gt; – AI-powered business intelligence tool for non-experts.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select one of the assets listed earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review the README and deployment guides for assets relevant to your use case.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the repository and follow the deployment instructions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Customize the asset for your specific requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reach out to the AWS Frontier AI Engineering &amp;amp; Services team for advisory support.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cost of rebuilding is real, and it compounds. When teams rebuild RAG pipelines, recreate document processing logic, or start from scratch, those hours add up in engineering time that could have been spent solving actual customer problems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A curated, validated asset catalog changes the economics of generative AI delivery. Not by doing the work for you, but by making sure you’re not doing work that’s already been done. The 23 open source assets published since 2023 represent thousands of engineering hours, dozens of customer engagements, and countless lessons learned. They’re packaged into building blocks you can deploy today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assets are open source. The deployment guides are comprehensive. The customer’s success stories are real. Start with one asset that matches your current challenge. For expert assistance, reach out to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/innovation-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Accelerating product development with AI-driven lifecycle management (AI-PDLC) in AWS Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-product-development-with-ai-driven-lifecycle-management-ai-pdlc-in-aws-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vishnu Elangovan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 16:05:33 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Quick]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ca32afd4562f4691a5e0c8183feb87808df97a2a</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build an AI-driven product development lifecycle (AI-PDLC) with a suite of six AWS Quick agents that take you from scattered customer signals to a validated prototype across Discover, Decide, Design, and Prototype, with human approval at every gate and a specification ready to hand to coding agents like Kiro.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;This blog post focuses on how AI can improve and expedite the product development life cycle using agents built on AWS Quick, with human approval baked into the process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI-driven software delivery is helping teams ship products faster than ever. As implementation accelerates, the upstream product development process becomes even more important. Coding agents can turn clear requirements into working software faster than before, but they can’t compensate for building the wrong product. With implementation becoming easier, your competitive advantage moves upstream to four critical activities: discovering the right customer problem, deciding which opportunity to pursue, designing the right experience, and validating it through a prototype.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The signals that you need to make those decisions are scattered everywhere. Your customers leave feedback in Slack, your sales team logs insights in Salesforce, systems record incidents in ServiceNow, stakeholders document requirements in Microsoft SharePoint, engineers track work items in Jira, and teams make decisions in Outlook meetings and email threads. As your teams move between these tools, they disconnect context from the decisions it should inform, and lose more at every handoff.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Quick provides the connective layer for this fragmented knowledge. By grounding custom chat agents in connected enterprise data and storing their artifacts in shared &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/spaces/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Spaces&lt;/a&gt;, AWS Quick helps your team build with existing organizational knowledge instead of starting each initiative from a blank page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AI-Driven Product Development Lifecycle (AI-PDLC) is a framework that applies these capabilities through a suite of six AWS Quick agents with specialized skills, producing a traceable set of artifacts at each phase. Agents gather and structure context, surface gaps, and draft artifacts. You and your team contribute judgment and approve every decision. The objective isn’t to produce documents faster. It’s to help your team think better, preserve why each decision was made, and give engineering and coding agents a specification grounded in customer evidence. We will show you how to configure the six-agent AI-PDLC suite in AWS Quick and use it to move from distributed customer signals to a validated prototype. Throughout, it preserves context from the first insight through the engineering handoff.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The diagram shows the end-to-end flow of the AI-PDLC framework.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-1.png" alt="End-to-end AI-PDLC flow from enterprise signals through Amazon Quick agents to coding agents" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end AI-PDLC flow, from scattered enterprise signals through AWS Quick agents to the coding-agent handoff&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It starts with the signals most teams lose track of, that is, the Slack threads, Jira tickets, docs, and call transcripts that hold the why behind a decision and what customers actually said. Enterprise connectors pull that context from Slack, Outlook, Jira, Salesforce, SharePoint, ServiceNow, and many more enterprise sources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-2.png" alt="Amazon Quick personal knowledge graph linking people, projects, and decisions across connected tools" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: AWS Quick builds a personal knowledge graph that links people, projects, and decisions across connected apps and folders&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Quick can build a personal knowledge graph from your connected apps and folders, linking the people, projects, and decisions across your tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Those signals feed the brain: the set of agents on AWS Quick, led by an Orchestrator that runs Discovery, Prioritize, Working Backwards, Spec Generator, and Designer. Everything they produce lands in one common language: a &lt;code&gt;prototype_spec.md&lt;/code&gt; holding the acceptance criteria, personas, screens, data model. Every decision traces back to a source.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Quick can delegate development tasks to AI coding agents using the &lt;strong&gt;Agent Client Protocol (ACP)&lt;/strong&gt;. ACP is an open JSON-RPC 2.0 standard with no vendor lock-in. Customers can use their preferred coding agent like Kiro or Claude Code to build against the specification generated by AI-PDLC.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Underneath it all runs a human approval gate at every stage. You validate the pain points, approve priorities, sign off the spec, gate the build, and accept the review. Production data then flows back into Discovery, closing the loop. The agents do the work while you make the calls, and nobody has to leave their tool to do it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AI-PDLC lifecycle spans eight phases: Discover, Decide, Design, Prototype, Engineer, Ship, Operate, and Learn. This post focuses on the first four, where the product team works. The last four are handled by coding agents such as Kiro, which build from the specifications the upstream phases produce. The upstream phases are implemented as six agents in AWS Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-3.png" alt="The six Amazon Quick agents that implement the four upstream AI-PDLC phases" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The upstream AI-PDLC phases implemented as six agents in AWS Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users interact only with an Orchestrator, which reads each specialist’s question framework at runtime, asks targeted questions for the current phase, and dispatches artifact generation to the right specialist. A single conversation drives the whole journey, and the pipeline advances only after human approval at each step in the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-4.png" alt="Orchestrator-driven AI-PDLC pipeline advancing through phases after human approval at each gate" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: A single Orchestrator conversation drives the pipeline, advancing only after human approval at each gate&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-1-discover"&gt;Phase 1: Discover&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;Discovery agent &lt;/em&gt;guides structured pain point analysis across interactive Q&amp;amp;A, URL ingestion, and direct pulls from connected enterprise sources like Slack, Jira, support tickets, Salesforce, and research repositories. It scores every pain point for severity and frequency, producing a &lt;em&gt;Pain Point Board&lt;/em&gt; that gives the team one evidence-grounded view of what customers struggle with.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-5.png" alt="Pain Point Board scoring customer pain points by severity and frequency" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The Discovery agent produces a Pain Point Board scored by severity and frequency&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-2-decide"&gt;Phase 2: Decide&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;Prioritize agent&lt;/em&gt; scores each use case against weighted criteria using two transparent 0–10 frameworks: one purpose-built for agentic use cases (large language model (LLM) capability match, tool availability, user acceptance) and one for traditional applications (feasibility, business impact, effort). The team can open the &lt;em&gt;Scoring Matrix&lt;/em&gt; and trace exactly why one use case outranks another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-6.png" alt="Scoring Matrix ranking use cases against weighted prioritization criteria" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The Prioritize agent Scoring Matrix shows why one use case outranks another&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once the top use case is selected, the &lt;em&gt;Working Backwards agent&lt;/em&gt; generates a full PR/FAQ using the Amazon Working Backwards method with customer FAQs and internal FAQs covering unit economics, failure modes, regulatory risk, and competitive landscape. If a concept can’t survive this scrutiny, the team learns that on paper before committing engineering resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-7.png" alt="PR/FAQ generated by the Working Backwards agent with customer and internal FAQs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: The Working Backwards agent generates a full PR/FAQ for the top use case&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-3-design"&gt;Phase 3: Design&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;Spec Generator agent&lt;/em&gt; turns the validated use case and PR/FAQ into a build-ready specification, with separate templates for agentic versus application use cases plus a &lt;em&gt;Design Handoff Brief&lt;/em&gt; for UX teams. Each spec contains acceptance criteria, user flow, constraints, and metadata that traces every requirement back to its source pain point, prioritization score, and the PR/FAQ that validated it. Those acceptance criteria serve a dual purpose: they’re the build contract the coding agent follows, and the review rubric the review agent audits against. What used to take multiple sprints of drafting and review becomes a focused session, detailed enough for engineering to build from design spec immediately, with few or no clarifying meetings required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-8.png" alt="Build-ready specification with acceptance criteria traced back to source pain points" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: The Spec Generator agent turns the validated use case into a build-ready specification&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="phase-4-prototype"&gt;Phase 4: Prototype&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;Designer agent&lt;/em&gt; generates &lt;em&gt;journey maps&lt;/em&gt;, &lt;em&gt;wireframes&lt;/em&gt;, and &lt;em&gt;clickable HTML prototypes &lt;/em&gt;directly inside AWS Quick. It works from the Design Handoff Brief, personas from Discovery, user flow from the PR/FAQ, screens to design, brand context, and the emotional target drawn from the customer quote, so teams can validate concepts with stakeholders before committing to Figma or other design tooling. After the spec is approved, it’s dispatched to the engineering team for development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-9.png" alt="Clickable prototype and wireframe generated by the Designer agent in Amazon Quick" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: The Designer agent generates journey maps, wireframes, and clickable prototypes inside AWS Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The entire loop is iterative, with humans reviewing and approving at every gate. Nothing ships without explicit signoff. What traditionally takes multiple weeks of prototyping and iteration could become days or even just hours of guided agent collaboration depending on the complexity of the problems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every decision is logged at two levels. Each specialist records its own decisions, scores, and rationale inside the artifact it produces, and the Orchestrator independently checks that those entries exist while keeping a shared log across the whole pipeline. If one layer slips, the other still holds a complete record, so no gate approval goes untracked.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI-PDLC can be executed across two pacing modes. Deep Discovery asks 8–12 questions per phase, chasing ambiguities and edge cases. It fits a new problem space where the team wants thorough exploration. Fast Track trims each phase to 3–5 essential questions and suggests defaults from context it already holds, letting you confirm or override each one. It fits time-boxed sessions where the team knows the space and needs to move quickly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agent-implementation-details-step-by-step"&gt;Agent implementation details: Step by step&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We will walk through the details of the agent creation and implementation process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Quick workspace with the Desktop client installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to create custom agents in AWS Quick with Enterprise connectors configured for Slack, Jira, SharePoint, or other tools your team uses.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-10.png" alt="Amazon Quick workspace with enterprise connectors configured for Slack, Jira, and SharePoint" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: An AWS Quick workspace with enterprise connectors configured&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-clone-the-ai-pdlc-repository"&gt;Step 1: Clone the AI-PDLC repository&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full agent suite, reference architecture, prompt templates, and setup instructions are available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-AI-PDLC-using-AmazonQuick" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AI-PDLC GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository contains:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Agent instruction files for all six agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Question framework samples for each specialist agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configuration files and setup guide.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-create-the-specialist-agents"&gt;Step 2: Create the specialist agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In AWS Quick, create the specialist agents using the instructions from the repository:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 2.1: Download the JSON file from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-AI-PDLC-using-AmazonQuick/blob/main/agents/ai_pdlc_agents_full.json" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository for agent building&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 2.2: Go to &lt;strong&gt;Quick Desktop&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Customize&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Agents&lt;/strong&gt; → &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt;, then choose &lt;strong&gt;Create from File&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ml-21390-fig11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ml-21390-fig11.png" alt="Quick Desktop Customize menu creating an agent from a file" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: Creating the specialist agents in Quick Desktop with Create from File&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 2.3: The chat window opens up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-12.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-12.png" alt="Amazon Quick chat window open after agent creation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 12: The chat window that opens during agent creation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 2.4: The following six agents with instructions are created:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-13.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-13.png" alt="The six AI-PDLC agents created in Amazon Quick with their instructions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 13: The six AI-PDLC agents created with their instructions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Orchestrator is the central architectural component. It dynamically reads each specialist’s instructions at runtime:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-14.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-14.png" alt="Orchestrator agent reading each specialist agent’s instructions at runtime" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 14: The Orchestrator dynamically reads each specialist’s instructions at runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-configure-quick-spaces-artifact-repository"&gt;Step 3: Configure Quick Spaces (artifact repository)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a shared Quick Space for storing pipeline artifacts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open &lt;strong&gt;Quick Spaces&lt;/strong&gt; and select &lt;strong&gt;Create New Space&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name: AI-PDLC Artifacts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grant access to relevant team members. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-15.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-15.png" alt="Creating the AI-PDLC Artifacts Space in Quick Spaces and granting team access" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 15: Creating the AI-PDLC Artifacts Space and granting access to team members&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-run-the-pipeline"&gt;Step 4: Run the pipeline&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open AWS Quick Desktop.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select &lt;strong&gt;AI-PDLC Orchestrator&lt;/strong&gt; from the agent picker.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter: “Run the full pipeline” (or “Fast Track” for an abbreviated session).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Answer the Orchestrator’s structured questions as it guides you through each phase.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review artifacts as they are produced at each gate. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-16.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-16.png" alt="Running the AI-PDLC Orchestrator pipeline and reviewing artifacts at each gate" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 16: Running the pipeline and reviewing artifacts as they are produced at each gate&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-17.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-17.png" alt="AI-PDLC Orchestrator starting a pipeline run" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 17: Starting a pipeline run with the AI-PDLC Orchestrator&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can select the pace, Deep Discovery or Fast Track, based on your needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-18.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/05/ML-21390-18.png" alt="Choosing between Deep Discovery and Fast Track pacing modes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 18: Selecting the pace, Deep Discovery or Fast Track, based on your needs&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="watch-it-in-action"&gt;Watch it in action&lt;/h2&gt;

&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uGkf1NpzzEA" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;YouTube Part 1: End-to-end product lifecycle with AWS Quick and AI coding agents (51:49)&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="http://youtube.com/watch?v=lwYwAgdklYw" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;YouTube Part 2: Creating AI-PDLC agents&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;The ACP handoff to coding agents.&lt;/li&gt;

&lt;h2 id="ai-pdlc-scenarios"&gt;AI-PDLC scenarios&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We will walk through some common scenarios where the AI-PDLC framework can be applied:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scenario-1-new-product-development-starting-from-scratch"&gt;Scenario 1: New product development, starting from scratch&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a team identifies a new market opportunity or unmet customer needs, they face the classic cold-start problem: scattered signals, competing ideas, and no structured way to move from insight to action. AI-PDLC guides the team through the full upstream journey, surfacing pain points from real customer data and scoring competing ideas against transparent criteria. It also validates the concept through a rigorous PR/FAQ and produces a build-ready spec so the first line of code is written against a validated plan, not a hunch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scenario-2-feature-addition-to-an-existing-product-roadmap-driven-or-customer-driven"&gt;Scenario 2: Feature addition to an existing product, roadmap-driven or customer-driven&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Product teams continuously receive signals that inform what to build next: customer feedback in support channels, feature requests from sales, usage data showing dropoff points, or strategic priorities from leadership. AI-PDLC accelerates this by pulling those signals from connected tools (Slack, Jira, Salesforce, AWS Quick Sight). The framework structures them into a prioritized backlog with evidence-backed scoring and produces a spec that preserves the “why” behind each feature, so engineering receives context-rich work items instead of one-line Jira tickets stripped of intent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scenario-3-bug-fixes-and-incident-driven-improvements"&gt;Scenario 3: Bug fixes and incident-driven improvements&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a production issue surfaces through customer complaints, monitoring alerts, or support escalations, the response often skips structured analysis and jumps straight to a code fix. AI-PDLC brings discipline to this reactive workflow by capturing the root cause as a documented pain point, assessing whether the fix warrants a broader design change or a targeted patch. It produces a scoped spec with testable acceptance criteria so the fix addresses the underlying problem rather than just the symptom, and the decision trail is preserved for future reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each scenario follows the same pipeline (Discover → Decide → Design → Prototype) but enters at different depths. New products run the full journey, feature additions may skip deep discovery, and bug fixes may compress to a single focused cycle. The methodology adapts while the audit trail and governance remain consistent regardless.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The resources this walkthrough creates incur no cost at rest, so you can safely keep the entire AI-PDLC suite in place if you plan to run the pipeline again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To remove what you no longer need, delete the six agents under &lt;strong&gt;Customize → Agents&lt;/strong&gt; in Quick Desktop, delete the &lt;strong&gt;AI-PDLC Artifacts&lt;/strong&gt; Space (or just the test artifacts within it) and revoke any team access you granted, and disconnect any Enterprise connectors or coding agent links you added solely for this exercise. You can also remove the cloned GitHub repository from your local machine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bottleneck in product development has shifted. Coding agents have made implementation fast. However, the upstream work still constrains teams: discovering customer problems, deciding what to build, validating the concept, and producing clear specifications. Teams that compress this upstream cycle can gain a compounding advantage: they build the right product, faster, with fewer costly pivots.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI-PDLC addresses this directly. It provides a structured, AI-native framework, implemented as a suite of agents in AWS Quick that guides product teams through Discover, Decide, Design, and Prototype with built-in governance at every gate. AI-PDLC attributes every decision. Every artifact traces back to customer evidence. And when the spec reaches engineering, it carries the full context of why it exists, who validated it, and what “done” looks like.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The product team’s role isn’t to write code. It’s to make the right decisions about what to build and why, quickly and traceably. AI-PDLC makes that possible.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started-today"&gt;Get started today&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-AI-PDLC-using-AmazonQuick" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Follow the setup guide in the repository to create five specialist agents and one Orchestrator.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select “AI-PDLC Orchestrator” from the agent picker and say: “Run the full pipeline”.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>UAEF: A framework-agnostic system for closed-loop evaluation of AI agents</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/uaef-a-framework-agnostic-system-for-closed-loop-evaluation-of-ai-agents/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Samaneh Aminikhanghahi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 15:50:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7134bb29ee133c2d22189c4c963a132a593c00aa</guid>

					<description>The Universal Agent Evaluation Framework (UAEF) is a framework-agnostic system for evaluating AI agents. UAEF converts each framework's traces into one canonical format, scores them with 36 built-in metrics across 7 dimensions, and stores results so teams can compare agent versions and catch regressions.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Teams building AI agents on AWS use a range of frameworks, including LangGraph, LangChain, Strands Agents, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Each framework records agent execution in its own way, so the structure of messages, tool calls, and handoffs between agents differs from one to the next. Evaluation tooling tends to follow the same boundaries: teams build metrics, scoring logic, and result storage against a single framework’s trace format, and they must rebuild them when they switch frameworks or run agents on more than one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This coupling makes a basic development question harder to answer: did a change to the prompt, model, or tools improve the agent? Answering it requires consistent metrics across versions of an agent and a shared record of past results to compare against. Agent quality also spans several dimensions that a single accuracy score does not capture: tool calling, response quality, responsible AI, performance, multi-turn conversation, multi-agent coordination, and reasoning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we introduce the Universal Agent Evaluation Framework (UAEF). Customers across 12 organizations in the telecom, healthcare, and financial services industries used UAEF to evaluate their AI agents with one consistent set of metrics and compare agent versions before release. UAEF is a framework-agnostic evaluation system: it uses adapters to convert traces from each framework into a common format, then scores those traces with 36 built-in metrics across 7 dimensions. It stores the results in Amazon DynamoDB and Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and generates reports and insights that inform the next iteration of the agent. This post describes how UAEF works, provides a playbook for choosing metrics and preparing datasets, and points to runnable notebooks for each supported framework.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="uaef-one-evaluation-layer-for-agent-frameworks"&gt;UAEF: One evaluation layer for agent frameworks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;UAEF is a framework-agnostic evaluation system for AI agents. It takes the agent a team already has and scores it the same way across supported frameworks and runtimes. UAEF takes two inputs: the agent under test and a dataset to run it on. The dataset provides the inputs for each test case, and it can also include ground-truth answers, the expected output for each input. Ground truth is optional, and only metrics that compare the agent’s response against a reference use it. To evaluate tool calling, the ground truth must also include the expected tool calls for each test case: which tools the agent should call, in what order, and with what arguments. Everything after that happens inside the UAEF.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the path a single evaluation takes, from the inputs to the reports and insights. First, an adapter reads the agent’s native execution, its messages, tool calls, and agent-to-agent handoffs, and normalizes it into one canonical trace, so that the steps that follow are identical no matter which framework produced the agent. Next, UAEF screens the trace for personally identifiable information (PII), such as email addresses, phone numbers, and credit card numbers, before data is stored. The evaluation engine then scores the trace on a selection from 36 built-in metrics across 7 quality dimensions. When you persist a run, UAEF records it as an experiment in Amazon DynamoDB and Amazon S3: Amazon DynamoDB holds the per-experiment metadata used for listing and comparison, and Amazon S3 holds the full results. With runs stored, a team can compare an agent version against a baseline to detect regressions and generate reports and insights that explain what changed and why. Those reports close the loop: the team evaluates the agent, learns what regressed or improved, changes the prompt, model, or tools, and evaluates again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-21994-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/04/ML-21994-1.png" alt="Closed-loop data flow: an adapter normalizes a framework trace, the engine scores it, UAEF stores the results, and reports feed the next iteration" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: UAEF closed-loop evaluation data flow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The rest of this section follows that flow in order: the adapters that make UAEF framework-agnostic, the evaluation engine and its metrics, PII detection, experiment storage and comparison, and the reports that close the loop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="canonical-trace-and-adapters"&gt;Canonical trace and adapters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Adapters are what make UAEF framework-agnostic. An adapter reads the record of an agent’s run, including its messages, tool calls, and agent-to-agent handoffs. It converts that record into one canonical representation called the &lt;code&gt;AgentTrace&lt;/code&gt;, which is the only thing the rest of UAEF scores. Because of that conversion, UAEF works with the agent, whatever SDK it was built with (such as LangGraph, LangChain, or Strands Agents). It can run locally, behind an HTTP endpoint, or on a managed runtime such as Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with traces in Langfuse or not captured at all. UAEF provides an adapter for each of these, and they produce the same &lt;code&gt;AgentTrace&lt;/code&gt;, so metrics, scores, and comparisons stay consistent across every one of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A trace reaches UAEF two ways. If one already exists, from a past run, a log, or an observability store, it is handed to UAEF and the matching adapter normalizes it. If not, UAEF invokes the agent for you: given a runtime Amazon Resource Name (ARN) or an HTTP endpoint, it calls the agent on each dataset input, collects the response, and adapts it in one step. Either way, the output is the same &lt;code&gt;AgentTrace&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because every metric, mode, and report operates on the &lt;code&gt;AgentTrace&lt;/code&gt; rather than on a framework native format, the scoring that follows is identical no matter where the trace came from. That separation, normalization first and scoring second, is what lets a team compare an agent built on one framework against an agent built on another on a single scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="evaluation-engine"&gt;Evaluation engine&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The evaluation engine scores a canonical trace across 7 quality dimensions, each capturing a distinct aspect of agent behavior that a single accuracy number would hide. Tool calling measures whether the agent selected the right tools, called them in a sensible order, and passed valid arguments. Response quality measures whether the final answer is relevant, complete, accurate, and grounded in the provided context rather than hallucinated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Responsible AI measures safety, bias, toxicity, and resistance to prompt injection. Performance measures operational cost in latency, token use, and throughput. Multi-turn measures how well the agent sustains a conversation, including context retention, coherence, and goal completion across turns. Multi-agent measures coordination in systems of cooperating agents, such as task delegation and the division of work. Reasoning measures the internal logic of the agent’s chain of thought, including consistency and freedom from fallacies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each dimension is computed by one or more of 36 built-in metrics, and every metric returns a normalized score in the interval [0, 1], where 1 is best, so dimensions and the overall result share one scale. Metrics come in two families. Deterministic metrics compute a score directly from the trace and the ground truth and reproduce exactly on repeated runs. Large language model (LLM)-as-a-judge metrics score qualities that resist a closed-form definition, such as response relevance or safety, by prompting a judge model on Amazon Bedrock. You can also add your own custom metrics or draw on the RAGAS and DeepEval integrations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because judge metrics feed an agent’s own output back into a prompt, that content is treated as untrusted input: an agent that can influence its judge can influence its own scores. The rubric is sent separately from the content, and the content is escaped so it cannot break out of its delimiter and be read as instructions. Each judge response is parsed strictly and must cite a verbatim span of the content it scored. A score whose evidence is not found is rejected rather than recorded. Judge scores are evaluation signal, not a safety decision, and should not gate a security control. For runtime enforcement on the agent itself, apply Amazon Bedrock Guardrails in its own invocation path. The full model is in SECURITY.md.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="experiment-management-and-comparison"&gt;Experiment management and comparison&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;UAEF records each evaluation as an experiment, so results from different agent versions can be compared later. Calling &lt;code&gt;evaluate()&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;batch_evaluate()&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;persist=True&lt;/code&gt; creates a new experiment, identified by an optional name and objective, and writes it to two stores with complementary roles. Amazon DynamoDB holds one row per experiment with the metadata used for listing and comparison: evaluation count, the average score per metric, and an overall average. Amazon S3 holds a JSON file with the full evaluation results, including per-metric scores and the judge’s reasoning. Teams that handle sensitive data can turn on PII redaction for the stored copy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Python library also models a finer-grained structure. An experiment contains runs, and each run records the agent design under test: framework, model, tools, system prompt, and parameters. A run can also record a configuration snapshot and a Git commit. You can mark one run as the baseline for its experiment. This ties each score to the change that produced it, whether a new prompt, a different model, or an added tool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Comparison works on the stored per-metric averages. For each metric that two runs have in common, the comparison engine calculates the absolute and percentage change. It then classifies the metric as a regression, an improvement, or unchanged, using a configurable threshold with a default of 5 percent. Several runs can be compared against a single baseline in one call. The regression detector also assigns a severity to each regression: minor (5–10 percent), moderate (10–20 percent), or severe (over 20 percent). It groups regressions by dimension, which shows, for example, whether a prompt change affected tool calling but not response quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the web UI, the Compare tab lists stored experiments and filters them by framework or dataset. You select two or more experiments. The first is used as the baseline, and the UI shows per-metric scores with regressions and improvements highlighted. The UI calls the same library comparison engine, so results match what the Python API returns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="report-and-insight-generation"&gt;Report and insight generation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After an evaluation is stored, UAEF reports on it at three levels, each answering a different question. How did this run perform? Why did it score that way? How does it compare with earlier versions?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Results view.&lt;/strong&gt; When an evaluation finishes, the web UI shows a summary of the experiment: the overall score, the number of test cases evaluated, the framework, and queries that failed to run. Per-metric averages appear with a dimension radar chart and a metric bar chart. A table lists every test case with its query, the agent’s response, the expected answer, and the score. Expanding a row shows the full response with its dimension and metric scores. For a multi-turn conversation, it shows each turn with its own scores and the tools the agent called.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Detailed report.&lt;/strong&gt; From the results view or the experiment list, you can generate a statistical report on demand. UAEF loads the stored results from Amazon S3 and opens the report in a new browser tab. The report breaks results down by dimension and by metric. It shows confidence intervals and outliers for each metric, score distributions, correlations between metrics, a responsible AI summary, and test cases that reported failures. It closes with recommendations that point to the lowest-scoring dimensions and the metrics that vary most across test cases. The report is computed from the scores alone, without an LLM, so the same results produce the same report.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LLM-generated insights.&lt;/strong&gt; In the Python library, you can pass a report to a model on Amazon Bedrock for analysis. For a single run, the analysis covers strengths, critical weaknesses, hypotheses about root causes, and prioritized recommendations. For a comparison of several runs, it covers the overall trend, likely causes of regressions and improvements, the risk each regression carries, and next steps. These insights suggest what to change in the prompt, model, or tools before the next evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="two-ways-to-use-it"&gt;Two ways to use it&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can run UAEF two ways over the same engine. With the Python library, you can evaluate agents in code from notebooks, scripts, and continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines. You can use the deployable AWS service to evaluate agents through a low-code web interface. You upload your dataset, select a framework and metrics, run the evaluation, and review the scores. Both paths run the identical evaluation engine and write to the same stores, so a result does not depend on which path produced it. You can start in the interface and move to code without changing how your results are scored or compared.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="evaluating-agents-with-uaef-a-playbook"&gt;Evaluating agents with UAEF: A playbook&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Knowing what UAEF can measure is different from knowing what you should measure. A travel-booking agent and a policy Q&amp;amp;A agent fail in different ways, and scoring both on every dimension wastes time and Amazon Bedrock invocations. Use this playbook to pick the metrics that match your agent, then add more as new questions come up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;UAEF is open source under the &lt;a href="https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0"&gt;Apache 2.0 license&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/uaef/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awslabs/uaef&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repository. It includes the Python library, framework adapters, built-in metrics, AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) infrastructure for the deployable service, the web interface, and runnable notebooks for each supported framework. The &lt;a href="https://awslabs.github.io/uaef/Getting-Started/installation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Installation Guide&lt;/a&gt; covers requirements, installing from a built package, and the development setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/uaef/blob/main/uaef-service/README.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service deployment guide&lt;/a&gt; covers deploying UAEF as a service with the web interface, the config reference, and teardown.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h3 id="choosing-the-metrics"&gt;Choosing the metrics&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start from your scenario, not the metric list. Find the column that matches your agent and begin with the metrics checked in it. The &lt;strong&gt;Needs ground truth&lt;/strong&gt; column shows which metrics compare against a reference answer or expected tool calls.&lt;/p&gt;
