			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Build a daily dependency watcher with LangGraph and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore web search</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-daily-dependency-watcher-with-langgraph-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-web-search/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sundar Raghavan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 23:34:05 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e67f3a0f38da6d5f16c13370534db7c14abe780a</guid>

					<description>The Web Search API in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gives agents current, cited web results signed with the AWS credentials they already use. In this post, build a daily dependency watcher as a LangGraph graph that researches package ecosystems in parallel, keeps its rules in code, and checks every finding against its cited sources.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;AI agents increasingly handle work that depends on what changed today, such as security advisories, product releases, prices, and regulations. A model’s training data can’t answer those questions, so agents need web search. For production use, teams also need results they can cite, and control over which sources count. They want search queries to stay inside their cloud environment because those queries often reveal internal work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Web Search API in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/web-search-api.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is now available. It gives agents and applications current web results through a single API call, signed with the AWS credentials they already use. AWS serves every query from a web index that Amazon operates, and doesn’t send it to a third-party search engine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each result includes the page’s title, URL, publication date, and an extract relevant to the query. Requests can include or exclude domains, and limit results to a publication date range. On &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt;, or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime&lt;/a&gt;, your agent calls the API with its execution role. There’s no search API key to store or rotate. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-web-search-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; is also available through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. There, one administrator source policy covers every agent and &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP)&lt;/a&gt; client.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangChain&lt;/a&gt; developers, the AgentCore web search tool in &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/integrations/tools/bedrock_agentcore_web_search" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangChain’s AWS integration&lt;/a&gt; now calls the Web Search API. One function call gives an agent current, cited search results. The tool can also hold rules that the model can’t change, such as a date window, a source blocklist, and a search budget. It returns its results as data that your code can check. It works with LangChain agents, with &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph&lt;/a&gt;, the orchestration framework that runs them, and with &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/deepagents/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deep Agents&lt;/a&gt;, which adds subagents, a virtual filesystem, and context compression.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you build a daily dependency watch for a security team as a LangGraph graph on the Web Search API. Every morning, the watch checks whether anything new affects the open source packages and container base images the team ships. It researches three package ecosystems in parallel, while your code keeps the date window, the blocklist, and the search budget out of the model’s control. It checks each finding against the sources it cites and remembers what it already reported. Then you run the watch on AgentCore runtime, and offer it across teams through AgentCore Gateway. In our test, the three branches finished in 36 seconds, against 99 seconds one after another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model decides what to search for and writes the findings. Every rule that must hold, whatever the model does, stays in code: the date window, the blocklist, the search budget, and the checks on each finding. Figure 1 shows how these parts fit together when you run the watch from the notebook.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-1.png" alt="Dependency watch architecture: one run fans out to three parallel research branches that each call a Claude model and the Web Search API, then merge to a cited report" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: A daily dependency watch with LangGraph and the Web Search API, run from a notebook&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The numbered steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A run starts the graph with the watchlist and the watch window.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;fan_out&lt;/code&gt; starts one research branch per ecosystem: npm, PyPI, and container base images. LangGraph runs the three branches in parallel.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Inside each branch, the agent calls an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/anthropic/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude&lt;/a&gt; model in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. The model decides what to search for and, at the end, produces structured findings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The branch’s search tool adds the date window and the blocklist to each query. It calls the Web Search API with a request signed with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/reference_sigv.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Signature Version 4 (SigV4)&lt;/a&gt;. Steps 3 and 4 repeat, up to four searches per branch, in every branch at the same time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each branch keeps only findings that its sources support and that fall inside the window. When all three branches finish, LangGraph merges their findings and runs &lt;code&gt;keep_new&lt;/code&gt; once.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;keep_new&lt;/code&gt; checks the &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/persistence" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph store&lt;/a&gt; and records new findings, so nothing is reported twice.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;report builds the cited table of new findings for the security team.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you start, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account, working in an AWS Region that offers the Web Search API. The examples use the US East (N. Virginia) Region (us-east-1). For other Regions, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported AWS Regions&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS credentials for the identity that runs the notebook, from environment variables or an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; profile. That identity needs the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; permission from Step 1 and access to an Anthropic Claude model in Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The following Python packages: boto3 1.43.127 or later, the AgentCore Python SDK (bedrock-agentcore) 1.26.0 or later, and langchain-aws 1.9.3 or later. Install them with this &lt;code&gt;pip&lt;/code&gt; command: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;pip install -U "boto3&amp;gt;=1.43.127" "bedrock-agentcore&amp;gt;=1.26.0" "langchain-aws[tools]&amp;gt;=1.9.3" langchain langgraph&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Web Search and model calls are billed per use. For details, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore pricing&lt;/a&gt;. One run of the watch makes at most 12 searches.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete code is in this &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws/blob/main/samples/agents/agentcore_web_search_supply_chain_watch.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;notebook&lt;/a&gt;. The notebook runs every cell, including setup. The following steps cover the parts that matter for search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-1-grant-the-permission"&gt;Step 1: Grant the permission&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, give the identity you run as permission to search. The Web Search API needs one IAM action on one resource that AWS owns, so the statement is the same in every account. (For the full setup, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/web-search-api-get-started.html#web-search-api-permissions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Grant permission to call the API&lt;/a&gt;.) This uses the same permission that a gateway’s service role uses for web search:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "bedrock-agentcore:InvokeWebSearch",
  "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:us-east-1:aws:tool/web-search.v1"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-2-build-a-search-tool-with-fixed-rules"&gt;Step 2: Build a search tool with fixed rules&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now build the search tool that each research branch uses. The watch can’t let the model choose the date window, the trusted sources, or the number of searches. The graph also needs the returned sources as data, to check findings against them later. The web search toolkit in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;langchain-aws package&lt;/a&gt; covers all of that with its options:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;toolkit, tools = create_web_search_toolkit(
    region="us-east-1",
    exclude_domains=BLOCKLIST,
    published_after=window_start.strftime("%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ"),
    model_filters=False,
    max_searches=4,
    return_sources=True,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With no gateway argument, the tool calls the Web Search API. Every search carries the blocklist and the date floor, and a model’s own arguments could only narrow them. With &lt;code&gt;model_filters=False&lt;/code&gt;, the model sees only a query argument. The tool stops after four searches, and it returns the results twice: as text for the model, and as an artifact for the graph. Each branch creates its own toolkit, so the branches search in parallel. You can give the same tools to a Deep Agents subagent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-3-define-the-findings-schema"&gt;Step 3: Define the findings schema&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the search tool in place, define what each branch returns. Each branch is a &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;create_agent&lt;/a&gt; agent with a Pydantic schema as its response format, so it returns findings instead of prose. A finding has the package, a one-line title, and the CVE or advisory ID if the source gives one. It also has the severity that the source states, whether the source says it’s exploited, a recommended action, and the source URL. The notebook has the full schema and prompt.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-4-build-the-graph-and-its-evidence-check"&gt;Step 4: Build the graph and its evidence check&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now assemble the graph. &lt;code&gt;fan_out&lt;/code&gt; sends one branch per ecosystem with the Send API in LangGraph, so the branches run in parallel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before a branch returns, code runs four checks on every finding. The checks compare the finding with what that branch’s own searches returned:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The cited URL came back from one of the branch’s searches.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The source’s title or text names the package as a whole word.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Any CVE or advisory ID in the finding appears in that text.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The source’s publication date falls inside the watch window. The artifact carries each date already parsed, and a missing date counts as outside.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After all branches finish, &lt;code&gt;keep_new&lt;/code&gt; compares the surviving findings with a LangGraph store, and report builds the table. These checks catch an unsupported URL, a source that doesn’t name the package, an invented ID, and an out-of-window source. They don’t confirm severity, exploitation status, affected versions, or the recommended action. They also don’t prove when an issue was first disclosed: a page that the index dates this week can describe an older incident. For a production security workflow, verify findings against authoritative vulnerability feeds. Have someone review a finding before anyone acts on it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-5-run-the-watch"&gt;Step 5: Run the watch&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You’re ready to run the watch with a 7-day window. In our run, the three branches made 12 searches and got 80 sources back. They finished in 36 seconds of wall time, against 99 seconds for the branches added together. The evidence check dropped two findings: one whose source didn’t name the package, and one whose CVE ID didn’t appear in its source. None of the sources came from the blocked domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four findings passed the checks, and &lt;code&gt;keep_new&lt;/code&gt; reported two of them. Earlier test runs had already reported the other two, so the store held them back. Figure 2 shows the run in &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/langsmith/quick-start-studio" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LangGraph Studio&lt;/a&gt;, the visual debugger for LangGraph. Every row of the report links to its source, which the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/web-search-api.html#web-search-api-acceptable-use" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search acceptable use terms&lt;/a&gt; require for any output shown to users.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-2.png" alt="LangGraph Studio run showing three parallel research branches and a report of only new, evidence-checked findings, each linked to its source" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: A run of the dependency watch in LangGraph Studio. The three research entries on the right are the three ecosystem branches, which LangGraph ran in parallel. The report lists only findings that passed the evidence check and weren’t reported before, each linked to its source&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-6-run-the-watch-again-to-see-only-new-findings"&gt;Step 6: Run the watch again to see only new findings&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Finally, run the watch a second time to see the store at work. A scheduled watch runs every day, and the team shouldn’t see the same advisory twice. In our second run, the branches searched again and kept two findings, and the store had reported both before. So the report had nothing new, as Figure 3 shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-3.png" alt="LangGraph Studio second run where keep_new finds all results already in the store, so the report shows no new findings" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: A second run with the same input. The branches search again, but keep_new finds every result already in the LangGraph store, so the report has nothing new&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a scheduled job, swap &lt;code&gt;InMemoryStore&lt;/code&gt; for a durable store with exact key lookups, such as &lt;code&gt;DynamoDBStore&lt;/code&gt; from &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws/tree/main/libs/langgraph-checkpoint-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;langgraph-checkpoint-aws&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="add-web-search-to-an-existing-langchain-or-deep-agents-app"&gt;Add web search to an existing LangChain or Deep Agents app&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already have a LangChain agent, adding web search takes a few changes. Check these before you deploy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permission: add the Step 1 statement to the identity or execution role that your app already runs with. Nothing else needs to be created.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Region: the toolkit’s Region must offer the Web Search API, and it can differ from the Region of your model or the rest of your app.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool name: the tool is named &lt;code&gt;web_search&lt;/code&gt;. If your agent already has a tool with that name, such as another search provider, set a different name on the tool before you add it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rules and budget: each toolkit carries its own filters and search budget. Create one toolkit for each agent or branch that needs separate rules or its own budget.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Throughput: all agents in an account share its Web Search quota, so parallel branches add up. By default, an account can send 10 requests per second in each Region. For details, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/bedrock-agentcore-limits.html#web-search-service-limits" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quotas for Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Deep Agents, you define the agent with &lt;code&gt;create_deep_agent&lt;/code&gt;: a model, tools, a system prompt, and subagents. Pass the toolkit’s tools in a subagent’s tools list. Set the rules as toolkit options, because the tool applies them on every search, whatever the model asks. To check findings against their sources, as in Step 4, run that check on the agent’s output or add it as middleware. In our test, a Deep Agents coordinator delegated to a researcher subagent with these tools and returned a cited answer in about a minute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="take-it-to-production-on-agentcore-runtime"&gt;Take it to production on AgentCore runtime&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you’re ready to run the watch every day without a notebook, host it on AgentCore runtime. Runtime runs agents built with any framework, including LangGraph, without a rewrite. It offers two compute types behind the same APIs. MicroVMs, the default, give each session its own isolated microVM, so one run can’t see another’s data. Instances run your agents on AWS-managed &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2)&lt;/a&gt; instances in your own account. They fit multi-day sessions, GPU workloads, or several agents that share one instance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the limits of each, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-sessions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Use isolated sessions for agents&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-instances.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Runtime Instances and capacity providers&lt;/a&gt;. The &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws/tree/main/samples/agents/agentcore_web_search_supply_chain_runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Runtime sample&lt;/a&gt; next to the notebook holds the same graph as a Runtime entrypoint. Its policy grants the Runtime execution role the Step 1 permission and no broader access. Its README walks through creating, deploying, and invoking the project with the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agentcore-cli" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore CLI&lt;/a&gt;, on either compute type.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-4.png" alt="Architecture of the dependency watch on AgentCore runtime, with each web search signed by the runtime execution role and the runtime returning the report" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The same dependency watch hosted on AgentCore runtime. Steps 2 to 7 match Figure 1, with each search signed by the Runtime execution role. At step 1, a caller invokes the runtime, for example with agentcore invoke. At step 8, the runtime returns the report&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Runtime session state is ephemeral, so the in-memory store lasts only as long as the session. A daily job needs the durable store from Step 6, invoked on a schedule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scale-the-watch-across-teams-with-agentcore-gateway"&gt;Scale the watch across teams with AgentCore Gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Web Search API and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-web-search-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search Tool&lt;/a&gt; on AgentCore Gateway search the same index, and the same graph works on both. Choose by who enforces the rules and who calls search:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use the Web Search API when your own application calls search and enforces its own rules, as in Steps 1 to 6. It needs only one IAM permission.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use AgentCore Gateway when a platform team offers search to many agents or teams and wants AWS to apply one administrator source policy to every search. The policy sits on the gateway target, hidden from the agent, which can’t lift it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use AgentCore Gateway when MCP clients such as Claude need search, or when you want web search next to other tools behind one MCP endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The notebook’s optional Step 7 runs the watch through a gateway. It creates a web search target that carries the blocklist as an administrator policy, so the search client no longer adds it. This route needs two IAM grants on two different principals. The gateway’s service role needs bedrock-agentcore:InvokeWebSearch on the web search tool. The credentials your agent signs with need bedrock-agentcore:InvokeGateway on the gateway. Figure 5 shows the flow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/06/ML-22037-5.png" alt="Architecture of the dependency watch calling AgentCore Gateway over MCP with SigV4, where the web search target applies the blocklist before calling AgentCore Web Search" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The same dependency watch through AgentCore Gateway. The graph is the same as in Figure 1. At step 4, the search tool calls AgentCore Gateway over MCP, signed with SigV4. At step 5, the web search target applies the administrator blocklist and calls AgentCore Web Search&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To give each team its own policy, add one web search target per team, each with its own blocklist. This &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws/blob/main/samples/agents/agentcore_web_search_langgraph_walkthrough.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;walkthrough notebook&lt;/a&gt; shows that pattern. For more on this route, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-web-search-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Web Search Tool&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Steps 1–6 create no AWS resources. If you added the Step 1 permission only for this post, remove it from the identity you attached it to. If you ran Step 7, delete the target and the gateway to avoid ongoing charges. The notebook’s last cell does this when you set &lt;code&gt;DELETE_GATEWAY = True&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;target = gateway_client.get_gateway_target_by_name(GATEWAY_ID, "security-sources")
gateway_client.delete_gateway_target_and_wait(gatewayIdentifier=GATEWAY_ID, targetId=target["targetId"])
gateway_client.delete_gateway_and_wait(gatewayIdentifier=GATEWAY_ID)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The notebook leaves the gateway’s service role in place so other gateways can use it. Delete it in the IAM console when you no longer need it. If you deployed to Runtime, run &lt;code&gt;agentcore remove all&lt;/code&gt; and then &lt;code&gt;agentcore deploy -y&lt;/code&gt; from the project folder.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Web Search API in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gives LangChain agents current, cited web results with the AWS credentials they already use. The dependency watch shows how to go beyond a single tool call. Keep the rules the model must not change in code, and keep the returned sources as data outside the model’s context. Check what can be checked, and keep the citations in everything users see. When more teams need the same search, AgentCore Gateway applies one policy to all of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, open the &lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-ai/langchain-aws/blob/main/samples/agents/agentcore_web_search_supply_chain_watch.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;notebook&lt;/a&gt;. For reference, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/integrations/tools/bedrock_agentcore_web_search" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Web Search integration page&lt;/a&gt; in the LangChain documentation and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/web-search-api.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Search the web using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Web Search&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>The agent evaluation playbook: Built-in, DeepEval, and AutoEval on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/the-agent-evaluation-playbook-built-in-deepeval-and-autoeval-on-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Bharathi Srinivasan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 22:35:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8ef082e0d287956d1e252b1606af8d42f4e11b1e</guid>

					<description>When you deploy an agent, whether it is working is really several questions: did it finish the task, stay on topic, leak data, or skip steps? This post shows how to evaluate agents on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with built-in, DeepEval, and AutoEval evaluators, and how to match each behavior that matters to the right evaluator at the right level.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When you deploy an agent to production, one of the hardest questions is whether it’s actually working? The bar isn’t whether it runs. It’s whether it does what the business needs, at scale. Many teams will tell you they can’t really see this. They spot-check outputs, watch latency, and collect user feedback when they can get it. None of that tells you whether the agent finished the user’s task, stayed on topic across a long session, leaked personally identifiable information (PII), or quietly skipped half the steps it was supposed to follow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-1.png" alt="Iceberg diagram contrasting the surface signals teams watch with the hidden agent behaviors that decide success below the waterline" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Teams usually watch surface signals, but business outcomes are decided by the agent behaviors below the waterline&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agent evaluation solves this problem. What makes this challenging is that a good score on one metric can hide a failure on another. A support agent can resolve every ticket and still expose personal information in its replies. In this case, the agent is failing although it has a high completion rate. To get evaluation right, measure the behaviors that matter for each agent, at the right level of detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to do this on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. We use the AgentCore built-in evaluators together with the DeepEval and AutoEval evaluators directly from AgentCore Evaluations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="start-with-business-outcomes-instead-of-metric-names"&gt;Start with business outcomes, instead of metric names&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The most common mistake is to start with the metric list and pick the names that sound right. “Helpfulness sounds good, so we use it.” The result runs and produces numbers, but it gives almost no useful insight. The metric fits its label, not what success means for the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instead, start with three steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Define success in business terms.&lt;/strong&gt; For a customer support agent, that might be first-contact resolution. For a research agent, it might be producing a complete, well-sourced answer grounded entirely in retrieved documents. For a content moderation assistant, it means flagging everything that should be flagged and only what should be flagged.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Break the outcome into observable behaviors.&lt;/strong&gt; A customer support agent that achieves first-contact resolution will typically complete the user’s stated goal, stay coherent across turns when the issue requires clarification, keep responses within compliance boundaries, and correctly invoke backend tools like ticket creation or order lookup.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Map each behavior to an evaluator at the right level.&lt;/strong&gt; Trace-level metrics cover per-turn or per-tool-call behaviors. Session-level metrics cover outcomes that only make sense when you look at the whole conversation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-2.png" alt="Three-step flow from business success to observable behaviors to evaluators, illustrated with a customer support mapping example" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Translating a business outcome into observable behaviors and then into specific evaluators at the trace or session level&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This three-step process is the mental model that makes evaluation useful. The rest of this post is about what AgentCore gives you to execute it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-evaluator-catalog"&gt;The evaluator catalog&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore has a broad catalog of evaluators from three sources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in evaluators&lt;/strong&gt; are authored and quality-tested by AWS. You reference them by their ID (&lt;code&gt;Builtin.GoalSuccessRate&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Builtin.Helpfulness&lt;/code&gt;, and 13 more) and the service handles everything else. They are benchmarked for reliability and cover the common dimensions, so they are the straightforward place to start.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Third-party managed evaluators&lt;/strong&gt; come from &lt;a href="https://deepeval.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeepEval&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://www.braintrust.dev/docs/evaluate/autoevals" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AutoEval&lt;/a&gt;, two established open-source evaluation libraries. When you use them in managed mode, AgentCore hosts the code, picks the judge model, and runs inference on its own capacity, exactly like built-in evaluators. If your team already uses one of these libraries in development, you can run the same metrics in production without a separate evaluation service.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom evaluators&lt;/strong&gt; take a built-in or third-party evaluator as a base, so you can swap in your own model and modify the prompt or rubric.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-3.png" alt="Table comparing AgentCore built-in, third-party managed, and custom derived evaluator tiers by scoring logic, judge model, and AWS quality testing" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The three evaluator tiers trade AWS quality assurance for increasing control over the model and account that run inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After creation, a custom derived evaluator is used exactly like other ones.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-evaluator-landscape"&gt;The evaluator landscape&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore ships with a broad catalog of evaluators, so you rarely start from a blank page. Before you write a custom judge, check whether one already measures the behavior you care about. The following figure shows the whole landscape.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-4.png" alt="Catalog of AgentCore evaluators by source: built-in AWS evaluators and trajectory checks, DeepEval, AutoEval, and custom LLM-as-a-judge" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The full evaluator landscape on AgentCore across built-in, DeepEval, AutoEval, and custom sources&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in AWS evaluators&lt;/strong&gt; fall into five groups:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Response quality&lt;/strong&gt; covers whether the answer is accurate, grounded, and well-formed: &lt;code&gt;Correctness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Faithfulness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Helpfulness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ResponseRelevance&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Conciseness&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Coherence&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InstructionFollowing&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;Refusal&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task and goal completion&lt;/strong&gt; has &lt;code&gt;GoalSuccessRate&lt;/code&gt;, which looks at the whole session and asks whether the user’s goal was met.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool use&lt;/strong&gt; has &lt;code&gt;ToolSelectionAccuracy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;ToolParameterAccuracy&lt;/code&gt;, which check whether the agent picked the right tool and passed it the right arguments.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt; has &lt;code&gt;SkillSelectionAccuracy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;SkillInstructionFollowing&lt;/code&gt;, for agents that load skills: was the right skill invoked, and were its steps followed?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Safety&lt;/strong&gt; has &lt;code&gt;Harmfulness&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;Stereotyping&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Next to these are three ground-truth trajectory checks: &lt;code&gt;TrajectoryExactOrderMatch&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;TrajectoryInOrderMatch&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;TrajectoryAnyOrderMatch&lt;/code&gt;. They compare the agent’s actual tool path against the one you expected. Reach for them when how the agent got there matters as much as what it produced.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;DeepEval&lt;/strong&gt; provides managed open-source metrics in four groups:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Task and goal completion: &lt;code&gt;TaskCompletion&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;GoalAccuracy&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;ConversationCompleteness&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Conversational quality: &lt;code&gt;TurnRelevancy&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;KnowledgeRetention&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;Summarization&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool use: &lt;code&gt;ToolUse&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Safety: &lt;code&gt;Bias&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Toxicity&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;PIILeakage&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These metrics are especially useful for multi-turn agents. &lt;code&gt;ConversationCompleteness&lt;/code&gt; catches an agent that answers the latest message but quietly drops earlier requests. &lt;code&gt;KnowledgeRetention&lt;/code&gt; catches an agent that forgets something the user said three turns ago.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AutoEval&lt;/strong&gt; offers more specialized signals:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;Security&lt;/code&gt; flags malicious output, such as attempts to inject instructions or reveal system prompts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;Possible&lt;/code&gt; catches over-cautious agents that wrongly declare a valid task impossible.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;Humor&lt;/code&gt; matters for consumer-facing agents where personality is part of the product.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom evaluators&lt;/strong&gt;. When no evaluator in the catalog fits, you can write a custom LLM-as-a-judge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every evaluator’s exact definition, scoring scale, and input requirements are in the prompt template library in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/prompt-templates-builtin.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. That includes the judge prompt it uses, the trace or session level it runs at, and whether it needs a reference answer or an expected trajectory. You can call the &lt;code&gt;ListEvaluators&lt;/code&gt; API to get the full catalog and your own custom evaluators in one response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-dimension-evaluators"&gt;Multi-dimension evaluators&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each evaluator so far returns one score per trace or session. That works when you measure one behavior. But step 2 of the outcome mapping rarely gives one behavior per evaluator. A support reply must resolve the issue, follow refund and data policy, and stay professional. One judge that weighs all three returns one number, and you cannot tell which criterion drove it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A multi-dimension evaluator is a custom LLM-as-a-judge with a list of dimensions instead of one scale. Each dimension has its own name, instruction, and numeric or categorical scale. The judge reads the trace once and scores every dimension in the same call. AgentCore returns one result per dimension.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21893-5.png" alt="Diagram of a multi-dimension evaluator: one judge call reads a trace once and returns a separate result for each scored dimension" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: A multi-dimension evaluator scores several dimensions from a single judge call and returns one result per dimension with no composite score&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use a multi-dimension evaluator when your success criteria can fail independently, such as an answer that’s accurate but non-compliant, or resolved but rude. It also needs every criterion to be judgeable from the same evidence, so that one judge call replaces several near-identical evaluators re-reading the same trace. When you score the criteria separately, you can gate on each one. A release that improves tone but regresses policy compliance fails, and a single blended score would hide that.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keep criteria in separate evaluators when an established built-in or third-party metric already covers them, such as &lt;code&gt;Builtin.Correctness&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.PIILeakage&lt;/code&gt;. Also keep them separate when they need different levels or judge models, because the dimensions in one evaluator share a single level and model. Do the same when some criteria need ground truth such as &lt;code&gt;{expected_response}&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;{assertions}&lt;/code&gt;. Keeping the reference-free criteria in their own evaluator means it can still run on live traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-stacks-by-agent-type"&gt;Example stacks by agent type&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of the following stacks starts with two or three evaluators that best match the business outcome. Add the others when you find a failure mode the first set misses. Evaluators marked “needs ground truth” run in batch evaluation only, because live traffic has no reference answer or expected path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer support&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-step research&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Domain content (medical, legal, financial)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Business outcome&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Problem solved in one session, with no downstream risk&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Complete, well-sourced answer through a defined workflow&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correct, unbiased, secure answers within the domain&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Main risk&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Dropped requests, unsafe or non-compliant replies&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Right answer through the wrong tool path, or lost context in long sessions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A wrong answer. A wrong answer is worse than no answer.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Start with&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.GoalSuccessRate&lt;/code&gt; (anchor)&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.ConversationCompleteness&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.PIILeakage&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.TrajectoryAnyOrderMatch&lt;/code&gt; (needs ground truth)&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.GoalAccuracy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.KnowledgeRetention&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.Correctness&lt;/code&gt; (needs ground truth)&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.PIILeakage&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.AutoEval.Security&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Add when needed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.Toxicity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.AutoEval.Security&lt;/code&gt; (sensitive account data)&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.Helpfulness&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.SkillInstructionFollowing&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.AutoEval.Possible&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;ThirdParty.DeepEval.Bias&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Builtin.Helpfulness&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Derived evaluators&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Optional&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Use for trajectory and skill metrics if inference must stay in your account&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Almost always, so every judge call stays under your data governance&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="running-evaluations-the-three-modes"&gt;Running evaluations: The three modes&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore supports three evaluation modes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On-demand evaluation is the fastest feedback loop. Pass a session directly to &lt;code&gt;client.evaluate()&lt;/code&gt; during development or debugging. Every evaluator in the system, including third-party, works here.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

client = boto3.client('bedrock-agentcore')

response = client.evaluate(
    evaluatorId="ThirdParty.DeepEval.TaskCompletion",
    evaluationInput={"sessionSpans": session_span_logs}
)

for result in response["evaluationResults"]:
    print(f"Value: {result.get('value')}")
    print(f"Explanation: {result.get('explanation', '')}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Online evaluation attaches to your live agent runtime and samples real traffic at a configured rate. This is your production health signal. Built-in and third-party evaluators mix freely in a single online evaluation config.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Batch evaluation runs a defined evaluator stack against a fixed set of sessions: your golden dataset for regression testing before releases. Use this to confirm that a prompt change or model upgrade preserved quality across every dimension you care about.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All three modes use the same evaluator IDs. Switching from on-demand development testing to production online evaluation is a configuration change, not a code rewrite.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="putting-it-together"&gt;Putting it together&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Increasingly, the hard part is choosing which evaluators to pick. Pick the two or three behaviors that best show whether you reached your business outcome, and measure those first. Add an evaluator when you find a failure your current set missed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see the full catalog, call the &lt;code&gt;ListEvaluators&lt;/code&gt; API. It returns the built-in, DeepEval, and AutoEval evaluators in one response along with any custom evaluators in your account. Before you pick any of them, answer two questions: “What does success look like for this agent?” and “What behavior in the trace shows that it happened?”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Autonomous treasury agents: How Trovata built an AI-native solution on Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/autonomous-treasury-agents-how-trovata-built-an-ai-native-solution-on-amazon-bedrock-and-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kushal Mangtani]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 10 Nov 2026 18:11:08 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">090c81c3e9be757b6ab9880edbbf523fcf740c59</guid>

					<description>Learn how Trovata built autonomous treasury agents on Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, using an RBAC-secured architecture for bank-branded AI deployment and agentic workflows that move corporate treasury from manual operations to real-time decision-making at enterprise scale.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Corporate treasury teams face a persistent challenge: fragmented banking data scattered across dozens of bank portals, manual reporting trapped in spreadsheets, and legacy Treasury Management Systems (TMS) that can’t scale for modern enterprise needs. For treasury teams these manual processes create delays, errors, and an inability to make real-time cash decisions when they matter most.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata is an AI-native cash and liquidity management platform with direct connections to over 10,000 banks, serving enterprises globally from a single cloud-based platform. In 2023, Trovata launched the first generative AI built specifically for treasury. Now, with Trovata’s latest AI-native updates, they’ve evolved from delivering insights to deploying autonomous agents that act on behalf of treasury teams.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata’s customers have seen measurable results: $1M+ in annualized cost savings, over 5 hours per day saved per accounting team, a 77 percent reduction in annual TMS costs, and treasury reporting reduced from hours or days to under a minute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explore how Trovata built autonomous treasury agents on Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, the architecture secured with role-based access control (RBAC) that supports bank-branded AI deployment, and how agentic workflows are transforming corporate treasury from manual operations to real-time decision-making at enterprise scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata operates as an AI-native treasury operating system, providing direct connections to over 10,000 banks through a single system for cash reporting, forecasting, analysis, and money movement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Treasury teams today face a three-pressure problem:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fragmented banking data&lt;/strong&gt; across multiple portals, requiring manual aggregation and reconciliation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual reporting and reconciliation&lt;/strong&gt; consuming hours or even days of skilled team members’ time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Legacy TMS applications&lt;/strong&gt; that lack cloud-native architecture and AI capabilities needed for modern operations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata’s evolution from its first generative AI for treasury in 2023 to its AI-native capabilities with autonomous agents represents a fundamental shift. The solution moves from static insights to persistent agentic workflows that operate continuously on behalf of treasury teams. Critically, Trovata AI is built directly into the treasury operating system. It’s not a generic chatbot layered on top, but embedded intelligence tied to real treasury objects with a governed path to automation through agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata chose AWS for this transformation for three reasons. Its serverless, cloud-native infrastructure supports real-time execution, Amazon Bedrock provides large language model (LLM) inference for natural language queries and AI features, and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore handles agent orchestration with RBAC security, rapid deployment, and global scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The core AWS services powering Trovata’s AI-native solution include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt; – LLM inference for natural language understanding and generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt; – Agent orchestration, agentic workflows, and RBAC security.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Serverless AWS infrastructure&lt;/strong&gt; – Real-time execution at enterprise scale.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-diagram"&gt;Architecture diagram&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Trovata AI architecture centers on autonomous treasury agents operating within a bank-branded, RBAC-secured framework. The solution comprises several key components working together:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trovata AI Chat&lt;/strong&gt; – Natural language querying of multi-bank treasury data, so users can ask questions like &lt;em&gt;“What was our net cash position across all accounts yesterday?”&lt;/em&gt; and receive instant, accurate responses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AI Insights&lt;/strong&gt; – Automated anomaly detection, variance explanation, and trend analysis that surfaces critical information proactively.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AI Agents&lt;/strong&gt; – Persistent workflows running on schedules, triggers, or rules to monitor activity, explain variance, detect anomalies, and deliver updates automatically, without human intervention.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent Builder-Configurator&lt;/strong&gt; – A management console for building and configuring treasury agents, giving teams full control over agent behavior and scope.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enterprise RBAC&lt;/strong&gt; – Role-based access control securing agentic workflows, making sure that agents operate within the same permission boundaries as human users.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-22181-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-22181-1.png" alt="Trovata AI architecture on Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore, linking a bank-branded portal and more than 10,000 banks to the AI Chat, AI Insights, and AI Agents layers" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Trovata AI architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, from the bank-branded portal through to multi-bank connectivity&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-implementation"&gt;Technical implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata built its AI-native solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, combining autonomous agents, natural language querying, and enterprise access controls. The following sections describe what the team built, the primary use cases, and the AgentCore capabilities behind it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-trovata-built-ai-native-solution"&gt;What Trovata built: AI-native solution&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata AI delivers autonomous treasury management agents embedded directly within the Trovata treasury operating system. These AI agents turn repeatable operational work into persistent workflows that run continuously. This is the work that consumes hours of skilled professionals’ time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents operate on schedules, triggers, or rules to monitor activity, explain variance, detect anomalies, and deliver updates automatically. The entire system runs on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a secure, isolated environment purpose-built for enterprise AI. When a user asks a question, the AI retrieves data through Trovata’s own verified API layer, analyzes it, and presents the response within that secure infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="primary-use-cases"&gt;Primary use cases&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cash forecasting&lt;/strong&gt; – Automated cash flow forecasting across multi-bank environments with scenario modeling, replacing manual spreadsheet-based projections.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Natural language querying&lt;/strong&gt; – Enterprise users query bank data in plain English. For example: &lt;em&gt;“What was our net cash position across all accounts yesterday?”&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Anomaly detection&lt;/strong&gt; – Real-time detection of unusual transactions, balance changes, or cash flow patterns that might indicate errors, fraud, or operational issues.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automated reconciliation&lt;/strong&gt; – AI-powered reconciliation summaries across hundreds of accounts, reducing what previously took hours to seconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agentic workflows&lt;/strong&gt; – Persistent agents that run autonomously by monitoring, analyzing, and reporting without human intervention on an ongoing basis.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ten-agent-patterns-from-real-customer-deployments"&gt;Ten agent patterns from real customer deployments&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Treasury teams have already started building AI agents in production. Trovata’s AI Agents Playbook provides a working library of ten agent patterns drawn from real customer deployments, covering the most common and high-value treasury workflows. Most treasury teams have 5–15 workflows that are ready for AI agents today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-capabilities-trovata-uses"&gt;AgentCore capabilities Trovata uses&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata’s implementation takes advantage of several key Amazon Bedrock AgentCore capabilities:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent orchestration&lt;/strong&gt; – Multi-step agentic workflows for complex treasury operations that require sequential reasoning and action.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RBAC security&lt;/strong&gt; – Role-based access control securing agent actions, making sure agents respect organizational permission boundaries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Real-time execution&lt;/strong&gt; – Serverless infrastructure that delivers sub-minute treasury reporting across hundreds of bank accounts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Bank-branded deployment&lt;/strong&gt; – Agents embedded within bank-branded tools, so banks can offer AI-powered treasury capabilities without needing to build AI themselves.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To explore a similar architecture, you will need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Familiarity with treasury management operations (cash forecasting, reconciliation, multi-bank connectivity)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Understanding of agentic AI architectures and enterprise RBAC models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Knowledge of financial services compliance requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you deployed any example resources while exploring this architecture, remember to delete them to avoid incurring future costs. Remove any Bedrock agents, AgentCore configurations, and associated infrastructure that were created for testing purposes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Trovata’s journey from manual treasury operations to autonomous AI-powered treasury management demonstrates the transformative potential of agentic AI in financial services. With over $1M in annualized cost savings, over 5 hours per day saved per accounting team, a 77 percent reduction in annual TMS costs, and reporting reduced from hours or days to under a minute, the impact is measurable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bank-branded deployment model is especially effective. Banks and enterprises get production-ready AI without building it themselves. Trovata AI shows how, with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, independent software vendors (ISVs) can embed governed, autonomous AI agents directly into vertical software as a service (SaaS) applications, delivering AI with the security and compliance-awareness that financial services demands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Looking ahead, Trovata plans to expand its agent patterns, deepen enterprise resource planning (ERP) integrations, and extend global bank connectivity. The company continues to advance what autonomous treasury management can achieve.&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://trovata.io/guides/ai-agents-playbook" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Trovata AI Agents Playbook&lt;/a&gt; – Ten agent patterns from real customer deployments.&lt;/li&gt;
